O as a matter of routine administration. Flt Comds shall ensure their budget 
allows for the recommended RON expenses.
b. Staff: Same day and RON Out-and-Back missions are recommended by Flt Comd 
and approved by Cmdt. They should be scheduled on a non-interference bases to 
the flying program. Out-and-Back missions are normally restricted in range to the 
prairie region (Manitoba, Saskatchewan, and Alberta), and normally restricted to 
one leg each way. The number of legs can be increased for weekend training with 
Cmdt’s approval.
6. Air Displays. Air displays and flypasts require Cmdt approval.

68
7. Designated Authorization Officer (DAO). Unless restricted by the Cmdt, all 2 CFFTS 
QFIs are appointed as DAOs for missions approved as per para 3 and Table 1. Harvard qualified 
pilots on strength with other 2 Cdn Air Div units are approved by the Cmdt as DAOs for 
missions approved as per para 3. All DAOs must have signed the "Assignment and 
Acknowledgement of Accountability" form (Annex E). The original copy will be retained on the 
QFI's training file in Standards.
8. The Duty Ops O will ensure that the current operational situation (such as weather, birds, 
PIREPs, and nav aid status) is posted, and current on the FlightPro Status Board. It is, however, 
ultimately the Aircraft Commander or Formation Lead’s responsibility determine if the sortie 
will be flown given the environmental factors and to ensure that the crew is briefed on the most 
current operational situation to include alternate airfields.
9. Flight Authorization - Restrictions. The following situations restrict Designated 
Authorization Officers from self-authorising and outline the procedure to be followed:
a. Formation. The Formation Comd will authorise all aircraft in the formation. With 
respect to Phase II & III formation tests, authorization remains the same, however 
the formation commander will defer to the Testing Officer for all aspects of 
mission conduct;
b. Student Solo/Mutual. Only personnel appointed as Senior QFI (B+) or those 
designated by the Flt Comd may authorize the initial CH syllabus solo mission 
and the NT Phase III syllabus solo mission, regardless of the phase of training. A 
DAO must authorize all other student solo/mutual missions;
c. Short Notice Schedule Changes. All missions shall be scheduled in FlightPro. In 
the event of a short notice change (i.e. mission change, crew change, additions, 
cancellations), FlightPro shall be updated to reflect the modified plan and a 
“Changed” status will be added to the schedule to notify crews;
d. Duty Ops O. When flight authorization has been provided through a flight 
Authorization Form, the Duty Ops O will ensure that the “Sign Out” has been 
correctly prepared with the proposed Flight details and will then authorise.
Authorized Manoeuvres and Advanced Handling Procedures
10. Except in an emergency or when specifically authorized by the Cmdt 2CFFTS, Staff and 
Students are prohibited from performing manoeuvres not defined in the SMM (non-standard 
maneuvers). Non-standard sequences comprised of SMM manoeuvres are authorized to be 
conducted during A Category training, only by delegated A Cat QFIs listed in the applicable A 
Cat training order.
Flight Operations and Scheduling SOPS

69
11. To promote more predictability in the schedule of QFIs at 2 CFFTS, scheduling for IPs 
has been centralized. The QFI Centric model of scheduling is based on QFI’s active participation 
in the planning process. By knowing their future work pattern, QFIs can schedule work and
family related appointments and commitments in advance. The aim of this initiative is to provide 
a meaningful work / life balance for QFIs at 2 CFFTS.
12. The IP centric weekly schedule is available through the DND ECN network. This 
scheduling calendar will be managed and maintained by the Operations / Scheduling Section. 
The window for scheduling will open 1 month prior and will close the Thursday before “going 
live”. Flt Comds are responsible for the supervision of their QFIs to ensure equitable scheduling 
amongst their staff. Any changes required after the schedule is closed must be made through the 
Flt Comd to the Ops / Sched section.
13. 2 CFFTS Ops will populate the next week’s FlightPro schedule per the QFI Centric 
schedule. Any short notice / same day changes to IP availability must have Flt Comd or delegate 
approval, and the affected QFI’s agreement to make the change. This ensures that QFIs maintain 
the ability to balance work and life despite there being unforeseen changes to the schedule. If the 
affected QFI is not able to flex to the new plan a spare QFI, as depicted in the QFI Centric 
schedule, will be used to complete the mission. 
14. No solo SP missions shall be scheduled in the first or last waves of the day unless a dual
backup mission is scheduled for that wave. This ensures that changes in conditions do not result 
in automatic losses of planned missions.
15. Each wave will have viable backup missions scheduled that can brief and fly on short 
notice for each wave. If a QFI is scheduled in a backup mission in relation to a student solo
mission planned in the first or last wave, they are expected to report and be ready to brief and fly 
if conditions inhibit completion of the solo mission. If the solo mission does launch, the planned 
QFI is free to complete other duties, physical training or other work activity. If this was the only 
planned duty for the QFI in the day they are released from duty providing all other aspects of 
their secondary duties, officer professional development and mandatory training are complete.
16. Schedulers and Flight Ops Os will generate the flying schedule for all Flights and FIS. 
Each Flight will order their strips within a wave in order of the Flight’s priorities. QFI slots will 
be filled according to their IP Centric availability.
17. Before cancelling a mission for personnel unavailability, schedulers are to contact the
QFIs in the spare slots or the schedulers from other flights to notify the spare QFI that they have 
been activated for a mission. 
18. Flt Comds shall monitor their staffing to ensure that members identified in IP centric are 
available for their designated waves. Prior to releasing unused QFIs in the last wave, all available 
aircraft missions should be dispatched or have reached the point where a mission cannot be 
briefed and flown prior to tower closing hours.

70
19. The use of supervised solos is encouraged if a SP or CM has been unable to fly the msn 
after several attempts due to restrictions in the flying program. 
20. QFIs that have future flying commitments such as APCs, SUP Checks, IRTs, will advise 
their Flt Training O or Flight Ops O with both the request and expiry date. This early scheduling 
will prevent the expiry of QFI qualifications.
21. A duty scheduler will be available 90 minutes prior to first launch, to make changes to 
the first and second waves. The responsibility to supply a duty scheduler will be rotated through 
the Flights and displayed on Flight Pro. It is the responsibility of the individual QFI to assess if 
their planned trip can go. If not, it is the expectation they will brief and fly another suitable 
mission and inform the duty scheduler. E.g. Low cloud prevents CH mission, QFI briefs and flies 
an IF mission.
22. Schedulers are to ensure identical formation callsigns are not used in consecutive waves. 
(ie.if Zeus flight is used in 2nd wave it cannot be used in 1st or 3rd).
Aircraft Captain Procedures 
23. Aircraft Captains prior to flight, shall:
a. ensure names, service numbers (if required), msn codes and reason for being on 
board of all occupants of the aircraft are recorded;
b. authorise the Flight details, signifying full understanding of the assigned duties 
and acknowledging that deviation from the Flight Authorization Form will not 
occur unless necessitated by an emergency or to comply with air regulations; and
c. review completed maintenance actions and electronically sign for aircraft 
acceptance using the Maintenix system. In the absence of Maintenix, the aircraft 
captain must sign the appropriate section of the Aircraft Maintenance Certificate, 
certifying that the aircraft state is satisfactory for intended Flt. Complete 
Foreflight weight and balance as outlined in Ops Note.
24. Aircraft Captains on termination of flight, shall:
a. sign in and complete the applicable details;
b. enter and log the air time as time from take-off to landing; and
c. add 0.1 hrs to air time for the calculation of flight time.
d. Aircrew qualified on type can record their flight time as per FOM Annex 
2.2.2.3.B in their RCAF Pilot’s Log Book.

71
Minimum Crew Requirements
25. The minimum crew required for all mutual cross-country missions will normally be two,
2 CFFTS staff pilots, unless otherwise authorised by the Cmdt. Normally, two staff pilots will fly 
on all local SCT missions. The Cmdt, DCO, CFI, 2 CFFTS Ops O or Flt Comds may authorise 
local solo SCT.
26. While on course, students are normally prohibited to fly with a QFI conducting SCT. 
Deviations from this Order shall be approved by the Cmdt/DCO/CFI. FIS CMs are permitted to 
fly on SCT missions. CMs shall obtain FIS Flt Comd approval prior to conducting any SCT 
missions with non-FIS QFIs.
Call Sign Allocation and Publishing
27. Supervisors are responsible for assigning callsigns to their staff and CDs are responsible 
for assigning student callsigns.
28. When a callsign is assigned or changed, the supervisor shall forward the revised callsign 
information to CAE Training Administration to ensure FlightPro reflects new call sign 
allocations.
Passengers
Refs: A. RCAF FOM
B. BGA-100-103/AA-00 15 Wg Flying Orders
29. Passenger flights in 2 CFFTS aircraft will be IAW the above references.
30. Only current QFIs are permitted to give control of the aircraft to passengers. Non aircrew 
passengers should not be given control of the aircraft below 1000’ AGL or during aerobatics.
31. Air experience flights will be mission-coded with an appropriate SCT code and charged 
against the staff pilot's SCT time.
32. The signed Passenger Form, which can be found in the 15 Wing Flying Orders will be 
presented to the Duty Ops O prior to the aircraft’s release.
33. The aircraft commander shall meet with the passenger one hour prior to stepping to 
deliver the passenger pre-flight briefing. This briefing is available in the public Flight Safety 
folder. Aircraft commanders are to thoroughly brief the following:
a. Overview of the planned flight and duration.
b. Canopy fracture and ejection seat handle location and operation.
c. Emergencies – Ground, take-off, and airborne.

72
d. Loss of communications between front and back seat – what PAX should 
do/expect.
e. Avoidance of control surfaces.
f. Procedures in the event of airsickness.
34. The aircraft commander shall supervise passenger strap-in and un-strapping and also 
ensure that all CBs, switches, and the ISS Selector are in the correct position.
35. After the passenger is strapped in the following items shall be briefed (these items may 
be shown to the passenger prior to strapping in, however the aircraft commander shall review 
and point the items out again after strap in):
a. How to adjust seat height.
b. How to adjust air vents for cooling.
c. How to adjust the radio controls and volume, as well as how to adjust intercom 
volume.
d. The importance of not moving or adjusting anything without consent of the pilot.
e. ISS Selector, canopy fracture and ejection seat handle location and operation.
f. Location and operation of the emergency oxygen handle and the OBOGS/O2 
system.
36. Passengers and 2 CFFTS aircrew on familiarisation flights in 2 CFFTS aircraft that are 
not qualified, shall be seated in the rear cockpit and cannot log any flying time.
37. Post-landing checks shall be accomplished while the aircraft is stopped to ensure proper 
implementation of aircraft safety controls. 
Mission Duration
38. 2 CFFTS aircrew shall adhere to sign out/chock times to ensure that the flying wave 
structure is maintained throughout the flying day. Additionally, all Harvard QFIs conducting 
SCT shall ensure that their down time (wheels on ground) does not exceed 1.4 to allow for taxi 
to meet the chock time of 1.5 hours from their scheduled take-off time. If aircrew are going to 
overfly their chock time they must coordinate through the Duty Ops O.
39. All Harvard syllabus missions, including Review missions and Extra Dual Training shall 
be completed in 1.4 hours flight time (1.3 air + 0.1 taxi), unless otherwise required by the 
specific mission in the ITP. Requests to overfly this time shall be made to the CFI/Cmdt. 

73
40. Deviations from mission duration for operational reasons (runway shutdown, diversion 
for weather, etc) do not require authorization.
Duty Not Carried Out (DNCO) / Ineffective Time Policy
41. The following outlines the DNCO/Ineffective time policy for all students/course 
members. As a guideline:
a. part of a mission cannot be completed because of extenuating circumstances, such 
as weather, unserviceability, etc., it is to be annotated DNCO/Ineffective;
b. every effort should be made by QFIs to maximize training value, and minimize 
DNCO/Ineffective time on all missions;
c. on dual missions, the QFI must be at the controls of the aircraft if the mission 
time is to be annotated as DNCO/Ineffective; and,
d. in a rare case where DNCO/Ineffective time may be warranted on a solo mission, 
it will be further discussed with the Duty Ops O and approved by the Flt Comd 
IAW the policy herein.
42. Recording of DNCO/Ineffective time:
a. Note the reason for DNCO/Ineffective time and clearly annotated on the 
student/CM mission progress card in FlightPro during the sign in procedure;
b. DNCO/Ineffective time shall not be recorded as Effective time;
c. depending on the amount of DNCO/Ineffective time, it is possible that a mission 
may be recorded as DCO, DPCO or DNCO; and,
d. after Flt Comd authorization, changes to DNCO/Ineffective time must be 
corrected in FlightPro (if applicable).
43. Authority. All requests for DNCO/Ineffective time, with the exception of Harvard 
ECTM, are subject to Flt Comd approval. Missions with DNCO/Ineffective time shall be 
submitted to the Flt Comd using the appropriate workflow in FlightPro. A reason for the 
DNCO/Ineffective time shall be entered in the workflow header box. All other unusual requests 
for DNCO/Ineffective time that are not covered by this policy shall be discussed with the CFI.
Weekend Flying Statement of Work
Ref: Statement of Work (SOW 9177) para 2.4.1.4

74
44. The 2 CFFTS Ops O will co-ordinate all weekend flying through CMAT and 15 Wing 
OSS after initial approval by Cmdt 2 CFFTS. A minimum of five days notice must be provided 
to CMAT as indicated in Ref.
45. The Moose Jaw MTCA consists of class A and B airspace above 12,500’ MSL. When 15 
Wing IFRCC (terminal control) is not active, the airspace is given to Winnipeg IFRCC. Aircrew 
operating inside the MTCA when Moose Jaw terminal is closed must abide by the rules of class 
A and B airspace and will require a clearance from Winnipeg Centre in order to operate above 
12,499’ MSL.
Weekend Flying Scheduling and Operations
46. General Policy. Due to staffing restrictions during APS and summer leave season, local 
weekend flying shall not normally be scheduled in the inclusive period between the June 1st and 
Labour Day long weekends, or any other long weekends. Additionally, local weekend flying 
shall not normally be scheduled to occur on consecutive weekends. Saturday shall be the planned 
day for flying and Sunday shall be the back-up. Only QFIs and students shall normally be 
required to report for duty during weekend flying.
47. Types of Weekend Flying.
a. Full Program. A full program shall include all available CT-156 aircraft, all 
available QFIs and it will use both runways;
b. Limited Program. A limited program shall only include one aircraft type and, 
depending upon ATC staffing, will likely be limited to single runway operations; 
and
c. Deployed. Deployed weekend flying shall see aircraft deployed, usually to 
Regina, to fly a minimum of two trips per day. Aircraft will normally deploy in 
the last wave on Friday and return either during a Sunday / Statutory Holiday 
recovery window or during the first wave of the work week.
48. Requirement.
a. Harvard II. Weekend flying shall normally be considered for the CT-156 fleet 
whenever the 2 CFFTS is required to meet the requirements of follow on courses.
49. Scheduling. Additional full or limited program weekend flying may be required due to 
the inability to meet sortie generation rates. 2 CFFTS Ops will endeavour to provide as much 
notice as possible; normally more than two weeks.
50. Notification. The Ops O shall ensure that all staff and students are informed of any plans 
for weekend flying as far ahead of the scheduled date(s) as possible. Notification should be done 
at the CUB.

75
51. Temporary Duty. Funding of the TD for Weekend Flying will be IAW the Temporary 
Duty section of the 2 CFFTS orders, and CFTDI.
52. Compensatory Time Off. In accordance with 2 CFFTS Orders, members shall be 
permitted to take NWD/CTO to make up for weekend flying. One day of NWD/CTO shall be 
granted to a member conducting the duties of Supervisor of Flying (SOF) over a weekend period. 
This can increased to two days for SOF duties over a holiday weekend.
Pilot Logbooks and Logging of Time
53. All 2 CFFTS staff and students are to maintain their logbooks IAW RCAF FOM Annex 
2.2.2.3.B.
54. To ensure standardisation in the monthly ruling-off of pilot logbooks, the following 
procedures shall be followed:
a. A line in red ink shall be drawn below the last entry for the month on both pages;
b. the Big 2 logbook stamp or the information contained on it, shall be placed on the 
left hand page and filled out (Note: the signature block is for supervisor’s
signature);
c. a line in red ink shall be drawn below the ruled off block on both pages.
55. Undergraduate pilots (students prior to course completion) will only log flying time on 
TP syllabus trips. Flying time on syllabus missions where instructor proficiency is conducted and 
recorded as such in FlightPro may only be logged as passenger time. Air experience flights (such 
as formation back seater, Snowbird rides, and flights to Winnipeg for HAI) may also be logged 
as passenger time only and not dual. These trips cannot be used to collect Casual Aircrew 
Allowance. For example, if an instructor flies 0.3 hrs of Proficiency at the end of a student 
mission to reset their monthly Instrument Approach Currency and the total mission time 
including taxi time is 1.6 hrs, the student would only log 1.3 hrs dual time in their log book and 
the QFI would only log 1.3 hours QFI time.

76
CHAPTER 3 - WEATHER AND OPERATIONS
Briefings - Meteorology and Operations
1. All QFIs, FIS Course Members and students shall attend a scheduled Met briefing prior 
to flying or watch the recorded local Met briefing. 
2. Each Flt shall have an emergency/AOI limitation briefing coincident with met briefings, 
and all Night briefings. All student pilots shall have attended a minimum of one stand-up 
emergency briefing per flying training day.
3. 2 CFFTS Ops O will liaise with Serco to establish appropriate Met Briefing times then 
relay those times to all sections of 2 CFFTS.
Duty Ops Officer
4. Duty Ops Os must be a winged pilot, normally a pilot with experience as a QFI, or an Air 
Operations Officer who has reached OFP, and approved by the Cmdt. Exceptions to this order 
shall be approved by the Cmdt.
Supervisor of Flying (SOF)
5. The intent behind SOF is to act as the Cmdt’s representative and maintain overall 
OPCON of 2 CFFTS flying operations whenever the Cmdt, DCO, CFI, or Ops O are not 
available. A list of scheduled pers and will be created monthly based on the SOF schedule 
available to all SOF members through DND ECN. Ops / Scheduling will populate the QFI 
Centric schedule with the appropriate names in the SOF positions and these names will be added 
to FlightPro in the SOF Duty strips.
6. Scheduled SOF pers are responsible to manage replacements once the schedule has been 
published.
7. As required, the duty period will consist of an A.M. Shift: commencing at first Met Brief 
until 1.5 hrs following (or until the Cmdt, DCO, CFI or Ops O arrives); and, a P.M. Shift:
commencing at 1630 hrs until the last a/c lands. When 2 CFFTS is night flying, the P.M. SOF 
shift will commence at 1800 hrs until the last a/c lands. The Cmdt via Ops may assign a SOF in 
any other period. 
8. The SOF is responsible to supervise the Duty Ops Desk Officer by making command 
decisions WRT: (1) appropriate flying programs; (2) WX check requirements; (3) diversion of 
a/c, fleet; (4) unit wide recalls of a/c; and/or (5) cease flying, as required.
9. These decisions shall be made using all available resources (Wx, AIS, FlightPro etc). The 
designated SOF SHALL NOT be flying and remain in the building during their time of duty
unless relieved (see para 7).

77
10. If the proposed schedule interferes with a SOF mbr’s obligation, it is their responsibility 
to find a suitable replacement for their scheduled shift and advise the Deputy Ops O to amend 
the schedule.
11. If for any reason the SOF is required to leave the bldg they must hand over their duties to 
another approved SOF and advise the Duty Ops Desk.
12. In the event of an incident/accident, the SOF shall proceed to the Command Post as 
A/Cmdt in execution of the Wing Emergency Response Plan (15 WERP), and shall notify the
Cmdt DCO, CFI, Ops O or D/Ops O as soon as possible. 
Weather Check Procedures
13. The Weather Check procedure is now governed by RCAF FOM Appendix 3.7.2.1.E.2 -
CT156 Harvard II Weather Chek Procedure. Pilots assigned to do so shall conduct the weather 
check as described therein. 
PIREPs
14. A PIREP should be given directly to the Air Traffic Controller of Moose Jaw when
conducting flights using the Tower or Terminal freq. QFIs are encouraged to transmit updated 
PIREPs to the Duty Ops O throughout the flying period.
15. A PIREP should be given to OPS (who will then relay to the ATC personnel) when
conducting flights within the area and VFR flights outside the controlled zone.
16. When conducting a weather check, the PIC should report to Air Traffic Controller of 
Moose Jaw as well as the OPS personnel to help maximizing, in a timely fashion, the flying 
program.
CT-156 Harvard Policy on Icing Conditions
Refs: A. T-6A-1 (NFTC) 133-590008-3 (Harvard flight manual)
B. TAA-OAA 2014-01e, Definition and Guidance for Icing Conditions, 26 Jun 14.
C. Operational Approval, Use of CT156 Weather Check Procedure, 18 Nov 2021
17. The intent of this section is to outline the 2 CFFTS policy on icing conditions with 
respect to the CT-156 Hvd aircraft.
18. Deliberate flight in “Icing Conditions” shall be avoided in the CT-156 IAW Ref A. Ref B 
states “Icing Conditions” shall be considered to exist when:
a. There are weather reports in which Icing Conditions are forecast;
b. Icing Conditions are observed or reported; or

78
c. Any form of frozen precipitation (to include all forms of snow, hail, ice pellets, 
snow grains, etc.) adheres to any aircraft surface.
NOTE: nil-light icing forecast only in the comment box of the GFA is not considered “Icing 
Conditions” IAW ref B.
19. Pilots shall not fly in any “Icing Conditions” listed in para 18 unless:
a. A Weather Check has been completed IAW ref C which invalidates forecast 
icing; or
b. Flight clear of visible moisture (all forms of cloud, fog, and liquid precipitation) 
will be maintained.
20. Ref B advises “Icing Conditions will be considered very likely to exist in visible moisture 
at a SAT of +5°C and below” but does not consider this a known “Icing Condition.” 
Accordingly, with no “Icing Conditions” per para 18 and only visible moisture at a SAT of +5°C 
and below, the following applies:
a. A Weather Check is intended to invalidate a forecast of “Icing Conditions” and is 
therefore not required to be launched; 
b. Pilots may fly in these conditions without a weather check being conducted, 
however crews shall consider temperature, duration, and planned profile to plan a 
flight which avoids prolonged flight in conditions where icing is likely; and
c. These conditions trigger the Flying Supervisor as the launch authority in MALA 
who will confirm that the risk of icing conditions is minimal or nil based on 
temperature, duration or mission profile.
21. If icing conditions are encountered they are to be reported to ATC or AIS as soon as it is 
safe to do so. 
22. A PIREP of no icing should not be used to negate “Icing Conditions” as defined in para 
18 above. To do so, a weather check must be performed per Ref C. The following points will be 
used as guidance when deciding on a flying program or sending a weather check. The primary 
weather sources will be the GFA and AIS Met, but all official weather sources should be 
considered:
a. The decision to send a weather check rests with the Ops O, CFI, or Cmdt. If all of 
these are unavailable then a Flt Comd may also approve the weather check. Careful 
consideration of all weather sources should be made prior to making a decision as icing 
could create a significant hazard to aircraft;
b. If icing is forecast to be greater than Moderate then a weather check will not be 
sent;

79
c. If freezing rain/drizzle is forecast in Moose Jaw and/or our flying area then a 
weather check will not be sent. We will remain on Standby until the condition has passed 
or has been cancelled by met; and
d. If aircraft pick up ice while flying then the decision to send another weather check 
will rest with the Ops O, CFI, or Cmdt. If, after careful consideration of all weather 
sources, it is felt that the icing is no longer present then another weather check may be 
sent.
Good Airmanship and safety of flight will remain the highest priority when deciding to fly in 
forecast icing conditions or conditions where icing is very likely to exist.
Unplanned Weather Diversion
17. If an unplanned diversion occurs during a local mission, the Duty Ops O is to be notified 
as soon as possible after landing. The Duty Ops O will coordinate the RTB with CMAT 
servicing. Doing so will ensure that the aircraft hrs are not overflown and that proper 
authorization is given to conduct the following mission back to Moose Jaw.
Hot/Cold Weather Operations
Refs: A. RCAF FOM
B. 15 Wing Flying Orders
18. Cold Weather Orders of Dress. The 2 CFFTS Duty Ops O shall recommend appropriate 
flying clothing commensurate with existing and forecast weather. Minimum clothing 
combinations for 24 hour survival are as laid out in Ref A Annex 4.2.2.4.A.
19. Hot weather operations. Aircraft commanders and flying supervisors are responsible to 
exercise their best judgment in managing heat stress in order to both promote flight safety and 
prevent heat related injuries.
20. The primary tool to manage the risk of heat stress will be the MALA, taking into account 
the Wet Bulb Globe Temperature (WBGT), mission profile, mission number, and overall state of 
the crew.
21. The highest risk of heat stress in hot weather operations corresponds to flying following 
ground operations above 28°C WBGT. For the purposes of interpreting the MALA, "ground 
operations" refers to all outside activities (step to the aircraft, walk-around, pre-start) prior to 
cooling system initiation. Once cockpit cooling has begun, crews should self-assess whether they 
are still subject to risk of heat stress.
22. IAW FOM 4.2.2.1, the Cmdt 2 CFFTS authorizes aircrew to forgo the normal dual layer 
clothing requirement when the ambient temperature is at least 25°C.

80
23. The Duty Ops O is responsible to liaise with SERCO AIS MET for monitoring the wind 
chill factor and enforcing restrictions as follows:
a. Up to -29°C wind chill, flying program is unrestricted.
b. Between -29°C to -39°C wind chill, no single aircraft low level navigation 
missions. Limited launches at the discretion of the Duty Ops O.
c. Below -39°C wind chill or when ambient temperature falls below -35°C, flying 
only permitted under special circumstances with Cmdt’s approval (e.g. crosscountry departures).
Flying Program and Weather
24. The Duty Ops O, having considered actual and forecast weather as well as the aerodrome 
status, shall be responsible for declaring the flying program. Additionally, they must ensure 
airborne aircraft have received a transmission stating the change of the flying program 
specifically when VFR to IFR or to Standby. This shall also include any changes to alternate 
status. 2 CFFTS Harvard Ops flying programs shall be declared IAW OpsO direction.
Student Solo Flying
25. Prior to authorising a student solo, a QFI must thoroughly brief the student on the specific 
mission and the prohibited sequences as outlined in Annex H. The QFI will then authorize the 
student’s mission in FlightPro. 
26. Student solo flying shall not be authorised if the wind is beyond solo limits. Additional 
restrictions to solo students are as follows:
a. Ph II students are restricted to day VMC and shall not conduct a “cloud break”; 
b. Ph III Harvard and FIS CMs may:
(1) penetrate a layer of cloud within the MTCA (maximum of 5000’ thick 
Phase III/ 10,000’ for CM) if certified in Flight Pro Course Comments as 
capable of doing so. Additionally, the forecast and actual ceiling must be 
at least 3000’ AGL to allow a cloud break down; and,
(2) operate between layers in formation if certified in FlightPro Course 
Comments as capable of penetrating cloud as a wingman.
Airspace Deconfliction Radio Calls 
27. The following comm plan has been implemented IAW WFO Area Deconfliction Plan: 

81
a. ALL aircraft operating on the south side of the parallel runways inside the control 
zone will monitor outer tower frequency;
b. ALL aircraft operating on the north side of the parallel runways inside the control 
zone will monitor inner tower frequency; and,
c. ALL aircraft will make position reports whenever entering or exiting any CYA 
airspace, including when operating along the border between CYA airspace.
Harvards will make calls on VHF area frequencies, as req'd.
Designated Ops Frequency
28. Pilots wishing to communicate with the Duty Ops O may do so on 340.1 (Ch 14 
Harvard). Pilots will normally monitor 340.1 when operating within the MTCA or on low-level 
navigation missions, unless using a dedicated formation frequency.
Designated Maintenance Frequency
29. To reduce congestion on the Ops frequency, all maintenance related communications 
shall be conducted on 253.6 (Ch 15 Harvard). To distinguish from the Ops frequency, all initial 
calls shall be prefaced with “Harvard Snags, Aircraft XXX or C/S”.
Aircraft Un-serviceability Maintenance Debrief
30. When an aircraft is unserviceable, the pilot will normally debrief the unserviceability 
with an appropriate CMAT Maintenance person.
Heavy Bird Program Restrictions
Ref: WFO Sect 2, Ch 3, Art 306
31. The heavy bird program is based on information received from ATC and pilots flying in 
the control zone. ATC will report either “Heavy low-level Birds” or “Heavy Aerodrome Birds”.
32. When ATC is reporting “Heavy low-level Birds” the following restrictions apply:
a. Undergraduate solo navigation missions will not be authorised;
33. When ATC declares “Heavy low-level Birds”, Harvard aircrew will fly navigation 
missions at a minimum altitude of 1000’AGL until the aircraft captain can ascertain that it is safe 
to fly lower given the actual bird activity in the operating area. Normal LLTRs apply and if 
minimum altitudes need to be raised or the mission aborted due to bird activity the aircraft 
captain is expected to do so.
34. Ops may further restrict navigation missions in specific areas of the LLFA based on 
PIREPs received WRT bird activity. For this purpose, the LLFA will be divided into “North” 

82
and “South.” These areas will be treaty separately in this regard and PIREPs will be solicited 
from each area as required.
35. “Light” or “Medium” low-level bird statuses called by ATC will not normally affect the 
flying program, however pilots are reminded to keep a vigilant lookout for birds at all times. At 
any time if the bird hazard poses unacceptable risks to flight, aircrews should raise the minimum 
altitudes and/or RTB as necessary regardless of the reported bird program. In this regard, PIREPs 
are essential for Ops to continually assess the associated risks and ensure crews can proceed 
safely.
36. Weekend navigation missions based outside of CYMJ during the Bird Migration season 
should proceed at 1000’AGL until the aircraft captain can ensure that bird activity will not cause 
an undue risk to safe mission accomplishment.
37. When ATC is reporting “Heavy Aerodrome Birds”, the following restrictions apply:
a. only missions which can be accomplished with one departure from the aerodrome 
and one arrival to the aerodrome will be authorised (e.g. early IF, cross-country 
departures, mutual flights to the area, Dual low-level Navs);
b. the preferred method for recovery is via overhead break or PFL for CT-156 
Harvard. All approaches including formations will be for a full stop landing. 
Multiple sequences upon arrival are prohibited unless flight safety or ATC 
restrictions dictate otherwise; and
c. no undergraduate student solos.
38. PIREPs are an important part of the system used to determine bird activity levels, and as 
such, pilots are encouraged to inform FTS Ops and Tower of observed bird activity.
39. In some instances, 2 CFFTS may decide to go to a “Standby Program” when bird activity 
is such as to pose a significant danger to aircraft operations. The Duty Ops O will announce this 
on 340.1. The Duty Ops O will request the tower to broadcast this information on guard 
frequency 243.0. In such an instance, aircraft may complete their mission if able, but are to carry 
out a full stop upon RTB.
40. The Duty Ops O will re-evaluate using PIREPS and ATC reports to make a decision 
regarding the program. Cancellation of the “Standby Program” will also be announced on 340.1, 
ATIS, and guard.
Simulated Runway Threshold
41. For the purpose of local student training, the simulated runway threshold for Harvard’s is 
defined as the start of the runway identification numbers block. This point offers a measure of 
safety during student landings (i.e. to prevent touchdowns short of the actual threshold). 

83
Runway Length
42. All Applicable TOLD performance charts, including 50ft obstacle clearance IAW FOM 
2.2.3.10, shall be referenced to determine runway length suitability when flying away from 15 
Wing. Minimum runway length for landing or touch and go shall be in accordance with the 
calculated TOLD data; however, no less than 3000 ft (Harvard).
43. All operations on runways of less than the minimum runway length are prohibited with 
the exception of training conducted IAW 2 CFFTS Flying Orders, and/or while conducting 
practice instrument approaches with no intention to land. This may only be waived by the Cmdt 
and must include completed TOLD performance charts (both takeoff and landing).
44. The minimum permissible CRFI for Harvard operations is 0.35 with no crosswind 
component. Aircrew are to consult appropriate charts to ensure adherence to crosswind/CRFI 
limits.
45. The Duty OpsO will monitor airfield and alternate airport CRFI/crosswind reports and 
inform crews as required.

84
CHAPTER 4 - GROUND HANDLING 
Engine Start Procedures
1. No aircrew shall start an engine without a start crew present (GPU or Battery Start) while 
operating from 15 Wing Moose Jaw.
Pin Safety Checks
2. On Dual missions, both aircrews shall check both cockpits to confirm all pins and the ISS 
selector are in the proper configuration pre and post-flight. The intent is to ensure both cockpits 
are secured pre and post-flight and cross-checked by both crew members.
Checks/Strap In
3. Harvard QFIs are to supervise their SP strap in for the first five airborne sorties inclusive, 
as well as well as the initial solo.
Windshield/HUD Care
4. Harvard. Helmets should be placed on top of the instrument panel or coaming during 
strap in. The helmet shall not be hung from the canopy latches under any circumstances.
CT156 Ejection Seat Adjustment
5. The intent of this section is to prevent accidental activation of the MOR Handle, or 
damage to the aircraft, while adjusting the ejection seat height in the CT-156 Harvard II, or in the 
FTD.
6. A ground incident occurred where interference between the lap belt and the MOR Handle 
caused the accidental activation of the MOR Handle when the seat height was adjusted. As a 
result, the following notice was issued by Martin-Baker: 
“Personnel must ensure that all straps, harnesses, buckles or connectors and any other 
item which may cause an obstruction are kept clear of all moving components of the 
ejection seat during the raising/lowering operation. 
WARNING: Failure to observe this or other similar warnings can cause death or injury 
to you and adjacent personnel.”
7. All 2CFFTS personnel should fully strap-in, and ensure there are no obstructions of the 
seat, prior to adjusting seat height or while using the FTD. If the seat must be adjusted prior to 
strap-in, crews shall ensure that there are no obstructions to moving components.
Lap Belt Security Check and Frost Fitting Inspection

85
8. The following is a recommended procedure to follow to ensure that your lap belt is 
secure and serviceable prior to flight:
a. Positively identify that the lap belt is secured by fully extending both sides of the 
lap belt before sitting in the seat.
b. Once the lap belt is engaged, look for the red tape or the green marker. To ensure 
the Capewell Quick Release portion of the lap belt (right side) is properly 
fastened check that the lock lever is lying flat. No red tape should be showing. A 
small green spot will appear on the front side of the Capewell buckle when it is 
fully engaged.
c. Tighten lap belt. 
d. Pull sharply on the top webbing of the left side lap belt to ensure buckle is 
secured.
9. The intent of the following paragraph is to outline a maintenance alert for the CT156 
Harvard II Ejection Seat Lap Belt Frost Fitting Inspection. Although the following procedure is 
to be used by maintainers and ground crew, it is an important note for aircrew as well.
10. Upon strapping into the aircraft if the lap belt fittings are stiff and/or it takes excessive 
force to connect the fittings, inspect the following:
a. Ensure the Load Spring is not broken (dislodged)
b. Ensure the two glide plate screws are seated and have not backed out.
WARNING: If either condition exists, the Aircraft (frost fittings) is unserviceable 
and shall be written up as such.
11. Any questions or concerns regarding how to conduct these checks, or about the lap belt in 
general, can be addressed by the ALSE shop.
FTD Strap-in Procedure for CT156
12. When performing training in any FTD, pilots shall fasten and tighten the lap belt in order 
to avoid damage to the cable and pulley system to avoid damage to the FTD equipment. 
13. While adjusting the seat height, ensure that no loose items or parts of the FTD jam 
between the seat and the side console panels.
Canopy Prop Strut Usage
14. IAW the flight manual, the sole purpose of the Canopy Prop Strut is to provide limited cockpit 
ventilation when the aircraft is parked. Its design was not constructed nor tested with the intent 
for pilots to use it as a tool of leverage to close the canopy. Damage has been noted to the canopy 

86
through unauthorized use; therefore pilots are to cease using the Canopy Prop Strut for any task 
other than what is outlined in the flight manual.
Adjustment of Harvard Rudder Pedals
15. The CT-156 TAA issued a Risk Alert Notification (RAN), regarding a potential risk with 
the components of the rudder pedal adjustment system. In response to this RAN the following 
mitigation measures shall be applied by all CT-156 pilots:
a. Pilots are to avoid adjusting pedals to the extreme ends of travel. For those pilots 
who require the pedals to be adjusted near the extreme ends of travel, it is 
imperative that after reaching the end of travel, the adjustment crank is reversed a 
minimum of one half-turn.
b. If pedals are found to be stuck prior to adjustment, or become stuck during 
adjustment, do not apply force of any kind to free them. Seek assistance from a 
technician.
c. During flight control checks, avoid excessive force against the flight control stops 
as this type of repetitive impact can cause premature wear on the components of 
the flight control system.
Checks/Un-strapping
16. Staff and solo students may conduct pre-T/O checks while taxiing. Post landing checks 
may also be completed during the taxi to the ramp, with the following restrictions:
a. undergraduate solo students shall stop the aircraft first and install the ejection seat 
safety pin; and,
b. the post landing checks shall be completed prior to entering the ramp area.
17. Due to risks associated with operating the aircraft while not strapped into the ejection 
seat, Harvard II aircrew shall remain strapped in until the aircraft has been parked and the engine 
shut down. Unstrapping from the seat while taxiing is not authorized.
Arrestor Gear
Ref: flight manual Cable/Barrier Limitations (4-12)
18. To prevent damage to any 2 CFFTS aircraft, maximum taxi speed over arrestor cables 
shall be 10 knots. Takeoff roll shall not be commenced before crossing approach end arrestor; 
and touchdown shall occur beyond an approach end arrestor. As described in the CT156 
flight manual, Harvard pilots shall taxi over arrestor cables at as slow a speed as possible and never 
with the main gear doors open.

87
Lining-Up Behind Other Aircraft
19. Pilots shall exercise caution when lining-up for takeoff behind other departing aircraft by 
leaving appropriate space between them and the aircraft in front to minimize risk from FOD due 
to prop or jet wash.

88
CHAPTER 5 - VFR PROCEDURES
Use of Local Aerodromes
1. 2 CFFTS use of local airfields less than 3000ft (Harvard II) in length shall be restricted 
unless the following conditions are met:
a. emergency use;
b. all approaches will be terminated and overshoots completed by 50ft AGL;
c. training only to be conducted to aerodromes with a hard surface runway;
d. for student training, the simulated rwy threshold shall remain at the numbers; and,
e. aircraft shall operate IAW procedures outlined in the GPH 204.
2. Solo students authorized to conduct operations at airfields other than Moose Jaw shall 
conduct Low Approaches only and shall initiate an overshoot no lower than 200 ft AGL.
Harvard Use of Hawk Practice Areas
3. Harvards departing to Wood, Sand, Stone, or Edge shall advise Ops of their intent and 
apply the following procedure:
a. Call watchdog, UHF4 or VHF4, for traffic in the destination area (and the 
adjacent Harvard practice area if applicable);
b. Transmit a deconfliction call on the appropriate area frequency while transiting 
through Walrus, Sable or Eagle; and
c. Call established in the low block (6000-10'000 MSL) mid block (10'500-15'500 
MSL) or high block (16'000-19'000 MSL) of the applicable area once outside of 
40 nm.
4. While operating in Wood, Sand, Stone or Edge, crews shall:
a. Monitor the adjacent VHF area frequency for deconfliction (Walrus for Wood, 
Sable for Sand/Stone, and Eagle for Edge);
b. Use their EFB own-ship marker to maintain within the area boundaries; and
c. Adjust Bingo fuel for the longer transit.
5. Harvards in Walrus, Sable, and Eagle should maintain inside 40nm with increased 
vigilance due to potential traffic in the outer practice areas.

89
Closed Patterns – ATC Delays
6. When told by Tower to delay a closed pattern, 2 CFFTS aircrew will normally:
a. maintain a climb;
b. maintain full power;
c. allow airspeed to increase until clearance is obtained; and,
d. not normally initiate the closed pattern farther than ½ nm beyond the departure 
end of the runway.
Low Approaches
7. Under normal conditions, when carrying out low approaches, aircrew will not initiate Go 
Around until abeam the landing threshold.
Gear Up Low Approaches (GULAP)
8. Unless specifically authorised in connection with an air display or 2 CFFTS deployment
low approaches without landing gear extended are prohibited at non-military airports.
Go-Around Initiation
9. It is the expectation that the Go Around from a Low Approach should normally be 
initiated no later than the mid- point of the runway, but no earlier than the arrival threshold. 
Ultimately, the point at which the go around should be initiated will be based on sound judgment 
and decision making with consideration for traffic de-confliction and energy management.
PH-II G-Warm Introduction on CH4
10. The G-Warm exercise shall be introduced to Ph II SPs on CH4 and carried out on all 
subsequent CH missions. The intent to is to better prepare SPs for "G" related aerobatic 
manoeuvres later in the course, identify any "G" related physiological issues earlier on, and 
allow QFIs to start stressing the importance of the AGSM earlier in the syllabus.
11. During CH4 to CH8, if QFIs notice that a SP is struggling during the G-warm and/or 
exhibiting any negative physiological effects from "G" they are to inform their respective Flt 
Comd post flight and make a comment under the course comment tab in FlightPro.
12. Flt Comds are to closely monitor the SP's progress and restrict any SP who has displayed 
consistent difficulty with "G" on these early missions to either Supervised Solos or non-aerobatic 
Solos on all CH area Solos as a risk-mitigating measure.

90
Engine Failure after Take-Off (EFATO)
13. Practice turn-backs to the reciprocal of the departure runway in the event of simulated 
EFATO in the aircraft are strictly prohibited. Simulated emergencies are not to be practiced in 
the aircraft.
Landing Gear and Flap Lowering Airspeed Call
14. Aircrew shall provide a measure of protection against inadvertent ancillary over-speeds. 
Prior to lowering the landing gear or selecting flap, the pilot flying will:
a. state the airspeed and ensure it is at or below the limitation;
b. state the ancillary to be selected; and,
c. only then, move the hand from the PCL/Throttle to make the ancillary selection.

91
CHAPTER 6 - IFR PROCEDURES
Instrument Flight Training
1. Students on local IF missions, or in transit through the MTCA, are not responsible for 
area management, lookout, or R/T with respect to area deconfliction, and traffic advisory. 
Conduct of the Missed Approach in VMC
2. The following guidelines shall be adhered to when conducting missed approaches in 
VMC conditions:
a. The student will initiate the missed approach procedure.
b. The QFI will take control of the aircraft when required to adhere to VMC 
restrictions. The QFI will complete any remaining or partially completed PTO 
checks. 
c. While the QFI is in control of the aircraft, the student pilot will follow-through on 
the controls, but will not manipulate any navigational or communications 
equipment in preparation for follow-on use.
d. At the end of the runway, the QFI will select full power, adopt the climb attitude, 
and roughly trim the aircraft before giving control back to the student.
e. After acknowledging receiving control of the aircraft, the student will manipulate 
navigational and communications equipment as required, and will fly the assigned 
missed approach instructions.
3. The intent of this policy is to ensure that SPs are manipulating kit while they are in 
control of the aircraft, thereby developing the skills of being able to maintain aircraft control 
while accomplishing the other necessary tasks involved in a missed approach procedure.
4. For missed approaches in IMC, the student will retain control of the aircraft throughout
Acceptable Utilization of GPS
5. When required, GPS substitution for the VOR is permitted IAW GPH204A article 431. 
RNAV Approach Discrepancies
6. During some RNAV approaches, the published track as found in the GPH 200 is different 
than the DTK provided on the KLN900 GPS database. This is due to out of date magnetic 
variation found at the bottom on each approach plate.

92
7. As a result of discussions with the Division ICP, current errors are approximately in the 
1-2
o
range and can be considered acceptable. Larger errors in the 3
o
or greater should be 
considered unacceptable and the approach discontinued, or continued only under VMC 
conditions.
8. The following procedure will be utilized prior to IF missions:
a. Start up the KLN900 and allow it to complete its initial self-test. 
b. Upon completion, it will arrive at a page that lists a DATE/TIME and BARO 
(altimeter setting) on the right hand side.
c. Confirm that the DATE/TIME listed is the current date. If the date indicated is 
older than the current date, consider the database invalid and write it up as 
unserviceable. Be aware that the expiry date acknowledged on the subsequent 
page may be still appear valid.
9. Aircrew are to remain vigilant and continue to confirm the approach waypoints, for the 
approach loaded in the KLN900, match the waypoints in the GPH200 prior to flying the 
approach.
CT-156 Harvard Standardized Flight Plans
10. In this section you will find information concerning the flight plans/waypoints contained 
in the Harvard GPS. This information is useful for 2 CFFTS flying operations, and, as such, 
aircrew must ensure that they familiarize themselves with any amendments to this information 
via AIF or other means before attempting to use this system.
11. Flight plans 16 - 25 and any associated waypoints are not to be modified without specific 
direction from 2 CFFTS OPS.
12. Flight plan (FP) 25 incorporates the boundaries of the training areas. Additionally, 
CYR303 (Snowbird Training Area), is included in FP 25. This FP contains 30 waypoints. 
13. Once FP 25 has been loaded into FP0, loading an RNAV approach will cause the GPS to 
request confirmation to delete the first five waypoints of FP 0. The specific message is, 
"DELETING WPTS 1-5 TO MAKE ROOM IN FLIGHTPLAN-CONTINUE". After selecting 
continue, the waypoints defining Walrus will be deleted in FP 0. If the area map is required after 
completing an RNAV approach, reload FP 25. 
14. The GPS display correctly depicts 30 waypoints, however the Harvard EHSI Map Mode 
can only depict 25 active waypoints. As a result, FP25 will only display Sable/Snowbirds and 
Eagle on the EHSI. FP 24 has been created to allow depiction of the training areas on the EHSI 
using Map Mode, however to accommodate the 25-waypoint limit, the Snowbird area is not 
included.

93
15. The boundaries depicting Eagle encompass a portion of the Regina training area. This 
training area is capped at 5000' MSL. As such, no conflicts should occur, however pilots must 
remain vigilant and avoid the Regina training area when exiting Eagle to RTB.
16. FP 21 - 23 contain the waypoints for TAC NAVs 1 - 3.
17. IFR Block programs can be found in FP19 for Runway 29 and FP20 for Runway 11
18. IFR routes can be found in FP16 for Regina and FP17 for Swift Current
19. While the VOR will be used to determine radial boundaries between areas, DME shall be 
used when determining any of the distance-based boundaries. For example, the Harvard areas 
start at the 15 DME arc which is approximated using 3 waypoints but the actual boundary is the 
15 DME arc. The Electronic Flight Bag may also be used to provide additional situational 
awareness with regard to the MTCA boundaries.
Icing Conditions
20. IAW flight manual Harvard II, flight into known icing conditions is prohibited. Should aircraft 
have an inadvertent encounter with icing conditions, all safe measures should be utilised to exit 
icing conditions. The details of the icing conditions should be relayed to ATC as soon as 
practicable to aid passing of hazardous conditions. If time permits pass the same information to 
the Duty Ops O to aid in program and mission profile management.
21. 2 CFFTS Flt Safety is responsible to have photos taken and kept for future analysis when 
any aircraft lands with icing still on the aircraft. A Flight Safety Incident Report shall be 
submitted for in-flight icing encounters that exceed the approved 3/8”.
22. All aircrew will receive a briefing prior to employing this procedure and annually 
thereafter normally in conjunction with the Cold Weather Brief. Post Winter, Flight Safety will 
coordinate the analysis of that winter’s Ice Transition Reports and present the findings and 
recommendations to the unit chain of command. Any lessons learned / policy changes will be 
presented to all aircrew at the next Cold Weather Brief.

94
CHAPTER 7 - NIGHT FLYING
Night Flying Program
Ref: Statement of Work (SOW 9177) para 2.4.1.3
1. The 2 CFFTS OpsO will co-ordinate all aspects of the night flying program. IAW the 
above Ref, the night flying program will be programmed not less than one week before it is to be 
conducted. A Supervisor of Flying (SOF) will supervise night flying.
2. Harvard aircraft should minimize non-standard requests to ATC during period of high 
activity.
3. Flight Commanders must ensure their staff have received instructional training at 
night prior to teaching the Night Syllabus. This training shall be performed by a B Sup or A 
Cat IP and should focus primarily on pattern work and especially night landings. The mission 
need not be a full instructional scenario. 
Night Flying Briefing
4. Flt Comds and Aircraft Comds shall ensure that a thorough Night Flying briefing is 
conducted, and that the following is covered as a minimum:
a. Ensure each pilot carries a flashlight.
b. Use of cockpit, navigation, taxi and landing lights.
c. Taxiing.
d. Take-off (on centre line; transition to instruments by departure end).
e. Method of joining the traffic pattern.
f. Ensure solo students are aware that they are to advise ATC or Ops O of any 
change in weather/flying conditions, or runway conditions and, if necessary take 
the appropriate action.
Harvard Night Flying Traffic Pattern
5. Unless conducting an IFR approach, Harvard crews should maximize the use of the outer 
pattern for VFR traffic patterns during periods of concurrent Tutor activity. Pilots should also 
maximize the use of uncontrolled aerodromes in the vicinity of Moose Jaw.
6. Pilots shall plan to depart at the scheduled T/O times. This is to minimize ATC 
congestion through a stagger IOT maximize the number of assets able to complete a mission.

95
7. In order to improve SA in the traffic pattern at night, and prevent traffic conflicts caused 
by aircraft significantly off pattern references, GPS waypoints for the OHB base leg turn have 
been created and included in FP24. These waypoints are labelled OUT11, OUT29, INR11 and 
INR29. These waypoints will be included near the end of FP24 according to the following table:
WSKY
ECHO
OUT29 N 50 16.15 W 105 30.06
INR29 N 50 20.17 W 105 27.89
INR11 N 50 23.04 W 105 36.58
OUT11 N 50 19.52 W 105 39.71
CYMJ
8. Placing the waypoints in the above order will result in the normal boundaries of the inner 
and outer runway OHB pattern being displayed on the KLN900 (if appropriately scaled). NOTE: 
Due to the smaller inner pattern, the base leg is not perpendicular to the downwind leg and 
should not be used for reference. Pilots should be aware that FP24 does not include the Snowbird 
area and thus should avoid Sable low if that area is active. The flight plan may need to be reloaded if an RNAV approach is loaded as some waypoints will need to be deleted and the order 
changed, resulting in changes to how the waypoints will be presented on the KLN900.
9. Pilots may also elect to use the OBS function and select the active base leg waypoint to 
assist with accuracy during the rejoin from the area and, once established in the traffic pattern, 
turn the track to provide guidance for the downwind leg. Pilots are reminded that vigilant lookout 
remains paramount and heads down in the cockpit is to be avoided as much as possible. The GPS 
should be used to build SA and must not become a distraction.
Night Formation
10. Night Formation flying is NOT authorised for 2 CFFTS personnel.
Regina Night Ops Procedures
11. Due to noise complaints in the vicinity of Regina International Airport at night, the 
following procedures shall be followed when 2 CFFTS aircraft are conducting night operations:
a. All pilots intending to conduct night operations at Regina Airport shall advise 2 
CFFTS operations no later than 1200 local time the day of; and
b. Pilots who amend their planned itinerary during weekend cross country trips to 
include Regina for night flying shall advise 2 CFFTS operations duty officer as 
soon as possible but in no cases shall this time be later than 12:00 local the day of.
Pilots are prohibited from conducting night-time training at Regina Airport unless 
2 CFFTS Ops has been consulted, or in the event of an emergency or a diversion.

96
Saskatoon Night Ops Procedures
12. To mitigate noise complaints resulting from night flying ops at the Saskatoon airport, 
pattern work on all runways may be conducted prior to 2300h local, unless otherwise restricted 
by ATC or NOTAM. After 2300h local, night flying ops at the airport will be limited to one 
takeoff and one landing. In selecting the type of approach to be flown, pilots should give due 
consideration to noise abatement and select a procedure which best avoids overflight of
residential areas at low altitude and/or higher power settings.
Night Landings
13. In the interest of flight safety and to prevent hard landings, night practice forced landings 
should not be planned to a touchdown. Pilots may continue the PFL past the low approach 
altitude but must initiate the go around prior to touchdown.
14. Practice lights out landings at night are not permitted. 

97
CHAPTER 8 - FORMATION FLYING
Formation Limitations - Mutual
1. The following limitations shall be adhered to:
a. Two-plane formation: excluding Fighting Wing/fluid manoeuvring, shall at no 
time include aerobatic manoeuvres. The lead shall not exceed +4 "G" during 
Fighting Wing/fluid manoeuvring, and +3 "G" while manoeuvring in close 
formation. Additionally, Lead will maintain positive “G” at all times;
NOTE: For the purpose of formation, aerobatics are manoeuvres exceeding 120 of 
bank and/or 60 of pitch above or below the horizon.
b. Formations of more than two aircraft are normally limited to 90 of bank, 45 of 
pitch above or below the horizon and 3 "G", except that:
(1) three ship VIC and four ship box formations may be flown to 120 of bank 
and 60 of pitch;
(2) Four ship Echelon formations are normally restricted to stepped turns 
away of 30 of bank. Can be increased to 60 for flat turns;
(3) lead shall ensure all manoeuvres are within the capabilities of all 
Formation members, including Phase 2 QFIs who may or may not be 
approved to practice the planned sequences; and
(4) Fighting Wing/fluid manoeuvring may be flown with up to three aircraft 
without pitch/bank angle restrictions. Formations of more than three 
aircraft may conduct Fighting Wing/fluid manoeuvring without any angle 
of bank restrictions but limited to a maximum of 60 of pitch above or 
below the horizon while adhering to flight manual “G” limits. In a large 
formation, the Formation Lead should take particular care to ensure that 
their flight path does not interfere with trailing aircraft.
c. Formation takeoff and landing crosswind limits:
(1) Harvard II – not to exceed 15Kts dry runway, reducing to 10Kts and 5Kts 
for a wet or icy runway respectively; and
d. Four plane lead certification is required to lead all formations of greater than two 
aircraft, and
e. Except while conducting maneuvers associated with a departure, arrivals (e.g. 
GULAP, Battle Break, etc) or fly-past, 4-ship in the Low Level (below 1000' 
AGL) is prohibited.

98
f. The minimum altitude for formation wingovers/aerobatics in fluid manoeuvring is 
3000 ft AGL.
Tactical Formation 
2. To ensure Tactical Formation (TAC Form) qualified individuals have sufficient 
opportunity to maintain proficiency the number of QFIs authorized to conduct TAC Form will be 
limited.
3. The TAC Form training rules will be part of all Formation briefings for flights involving 
TAC Form.
4. CT156 aircraft shall not pass within 300’ of one another during crossing manoeuvres 
such as cross turns and shackles.
5. Minimum weather for CT156 operations when conducting TAC Form flying shall be a 
ceiling of 2000’ AGL, 5 miles visibility and a discernable horizon.
Formation Briefing
6. A comprehensive pre-Flt briefing will precede every Formation mission. This briefing 
will include Lost Wingman Procedures, Emergency Contingencies, and a BINGO fuel state that 
will be determined by the Formation Lead considering weather conditions, alternate 
requirements and other factors pertaining to the safe recovery of the formation. A formation 
commander will be identified during the briefing.
Formation Take-off
7. Three aircraft may take off in Formation if there is a QFI in each aircraft and the runway 
is at least 150 feet wide.
8. Similar aircraft with different configurations may take-off in close Formation provided 
the aircraft equipped with heavier configuration leads. Under no circumstances will dissimilar 
aircraft Formation take-offs be conducted.
9. For interval take-offs, the minimum separation between single aircraft shall be five 
seconds. Minimum separation between elements shall be 10 seconds, or 500 feet and five 
seconds.
10. Formations of more than three aircraft will not normally penetrate cloud. Departures as 
elements of two aircraft should be considered when cloud penetration is anticipated. Refer to 
Wing Flying Orders for local procedures. Consideration must be given to filing separate element 
flight plans when deployed to other airfields and anticipating IMC.

99
Harvard Formation Touch-and-Go Landing
11. Formation Touch-and-Go landings are authorised for the Harvard provided:
a. There must be a QFI in each aircraft.
b. The manoeuvre must be specifically briefed.
c. The weather and wind must meet normal wing landing criteria.
d. No more than two aircraft in the formation.
e. The runway must be at least 150’ wide.
Formation Stop-and-Go
12. Formation Stop-and-Go landings are permitted for the Harvard, provided there is minimal 
interruption to other traffic and subject to the following limitations:
a. There must be a QFI in each aircraft.
b. Minimum 4000’ of runway, or greater as determined by TOLD calculation, must 
be available to complete the T/O.
c. No more than two aircraft in the formation.
d. The runway must be at least 150’ wide.
e. A normal approach and landing is to be flown. After #2 calls clear, the lead 
aircraft will use normal braking and come to a stop maintaining the appropriate 
side of the runway. Aircraft will then line up and carry out the Formation take-off. 
If the Lead determines that insufficient runway is available, the take-off is not to 
be attempted and the aircraft will clear the runway.
f. Requests for Stop-and-Go landings and requests for Backtrack should not be 
made during periods of busy flying activity.
Formation Approach Limits (Flight Planning Purposes)
13. Approach limits for the Instrument Rating of the least qualified captain in the Formation 
shall be used. The final decision to carry out an approach in Formation rests with the Formation 
Comd and should take into account pilot experience and the landing conditions expected.
Formation Commander and Deputy

100
14. The Formation Comd will be annotated in FlightPro. In three or four-plane Formations, a 
Deputy Formation Comd will be named during the ground briefing and should normally be 
flown as number-3 in the formation.
15. The Formation Comd is responsible for the briefing and conduct of all staff and student 
fourplane missions. While airborne the Comd may fly in any position but retains responsibility 
for the mission.
Formation Recovery - Three and Four Plane
16. Formations of three or more aircraft will normally split into elements if an approach in 
IMC is anticipated.
Formation Authorization Away From Moose Jaw
17. Any 2 CFFTS pilot away from Moose Jaw and having not been previously authorised to 
conduct formation must first obtain verbal authorization from the 2 CFFTS Ops O, Flt Comd, 
CFI, CStdsO, DCO or Cmdt. The authorization will include the designation of a Formation lead 
that will be responsible to ensure the Formation is properly briefed and the flight conducted in a 
safe and professional manner.
Formation R/T and Protocol
18. Philosophy. The objective of standardized R/T is to minimize radio transmissions while 
eliminating confusion in communications between aircraft in the formation flight. Strict 
adherence to the standard R/T contained in this document is expected. The following are 
examples of approved terminology covers most situations. It is understood that there may be 
unique situations not covered in this document and pilots are expected to use good judgement in 
these cases. Formation R/T to ATC units can be found in the Wing Flying Orders. Directive calls 
are to be acknowledged by all subordinate formation members unless aircraft manoeuvring will 
make it obvious that the transmission has been understood. If there will be an unusual delay in 
carrying out flight lead’s directives (e.g. in practicing station changes) a request shall be made by 
the applicable formation member stating the requirements (i.e. “Viper 31 from 32, request 3 
minutes straight and level for station changes” or simply “Viper 31, request…”). Circumstances 
permitting, flight lead may grant the request, and simply advise if the request can no longer be 
supported.
19. PRI/AUX Radios/Communications: In a move to align 2 CFFTS operations with the 
Fighter Force and USAF doctrine, Radio communications will be conducted reference a Primary 
radio (PRI) and an Auxiliary radio (AUX) radio. As such, for the purposes of Formation flying in 
the Harvard II,
a. the “PRI” radio refers to pushing up on the PTT (UHF); and 
b. the “AUX” radio refers to pushing down on the PTT (VHF).

101
20. Formation Callsigns:
a. IAW standard NATO Air Tasking Order (ATO) conventions, and to better 
facilitate discrimination of transmissions in multiple formation environments, all 
formation call-signs shall consist of the flight name (i.e. Cowboy, Viper, Argo, 
etc..) followed by two numerical digits, the first digit being flight-specific and the 
second digit reflecting the aircraft’s sequential number in the formation. The 
Flight names used at 15 Wing Moose Jaw are published in the WFO. The flight 
number (11, 21, 51, 91, etc.) should deconflict from other formations.
b. In a Bandit Flight two-ship Formation, the lead aircraft would be “Shooter 91” 
and the wingman would be “Shooter 92”. The Formation’s call-sign, in this case, 
would be “Shooter 91 Flight”. If a third and fourth aircraft were included in the 
Formation, their call-signs would be “Shooter 93” and “Shooter 94” respectively.
c. In time critical situations or when communications are made internal to the 
formation, the truncated version of the full formation call-sign (omitting digits) 
may be used: e.g. “Vipers, Abort, Abort, Abort”, or “Blacks, Echelon left, etc.;
d. for any Formations that will be in radio contact with controlling agencies other 
than 15 Wg agencies, GPH 204 Ch 4 call-signs shall be used in conjunction with 
the flight-specific two-digit suffix as designated above. Upon returning to the 
Moose Jaw MTCA those GPH 204 call-signs shall be maintained for the 
remainder of the sortie. It is important to note that the flight specific numbering 
system must be adhered to rigidly to avoid duplication of call-signs in these 
circumstances.
21. Tactical Lead (Tac Ld) Changes. The authorized formation Lead is the individual who 
authorized the formation to carry out the assigned msn. The authorized Formation lead will 
retain that overall leadership role for the duration of the msn however, they can assign tactical 
lead status (Tac Ld) to a wingman. When the Tac Ld is given to another formation member, the 
lead/wingman contracts change (including the deconfliction contract) and the Tac Lead now 
becomes responsible for leading the formation. It is important to note, however, that the 
authorized formation flight lead can still override the Tac Lead’s decisions at any time or take 
the lead back when they wish as they still remain responsible for the formation. The formation 
should not be renumbered airborne.
22. Common R/T – Local Area. The following has been adopted to standardize formation 
R/T with future fighter ops as much as possible. In those instances that are not specifically 
covered, the philosophy of brevity and R/T discipline shall be exercised.
a. Brevity Response: Abbreviated call signs (e.g. “Vipers” vs. “Viper 31 Flight” and 
“32” vs. “Viper 32”) may be employed during intra-flight communications except 
during PRI frequency changes, PRI check-ins, and TERMINATE or KNOCK IT 
OFF calls.

102
b. Initial Check-In (Hvd)
(1) Lead on PRI: “Viper 31 flight, check”;
(2) Wingmen on PRI: “Viper 32”, “Viper 33”, etc;
(3) Lead on AUX: “Vipers check AUX”;
(4) Wingmen on AUX: “32”, “33”, etc.
c. Frequency changes are normally initiated by the flight lead on the intra-flight 
frequency. 
(1) PRI - Frequency change of the PRI radio is assumed if the descriptive 
word “AUX” is not added to the frequency change direction: “Viper 31 
Flight, push 3”. Use of full formation call signs is required on PRI checkins (“Viper 32”).
(2) AUX – Frequency change of the AUX radio will use the descriptive word 
“AUX” to precede the frequency change direction: “Vipers, push AUX 
16”. Only tactical callsign position number is required on AUX for all 
check-ins and acknowledgments (“32”).
(3) When “pushed” to the next frequency / channel wingman does not 
acknowledge.
(4) Once established on an intra-flight radio, to minimize external 
communications, the formation is not required to check in the flight 
positions when pushing to another external frequency.
d. When given a frequency change from ATC, the Formation lead should push the 
Formation to the new frequency via the current ATC frequency as opposed to 
intra-flight frequency. This achieves two aims; firstly, it is more expeditious for 
the formation, and secondly, it is the formation’s acknowledgement call to ATC. 
Example:
(1) Terminal (CH4 UHF) “Viper 31 Flight, switch channel 6”
(2) Lead (CH4 UHF) “Viper 31 Flight, push 6”
e. G Awareness and Fluid Manoeuvring. Prior to conducting a formation Gawareness or fluid manoeuvring, a fuel check and tank balance must be conducted 
and verbalized. If the responding formation member has the same amount of fuel 
(+/- 30lbs) and both tanks are within 50 lbs of each other, the response is “same, 
balanced”. Otherwise, the formation member will respond with their actual fuel 

103
state and/or “heavy right/left” with the fuel imbalance amount above the 50 lb 
limit. This check ensures that manoeuvring can be conducted.
Viper 31 – “Vipers, standby for fluid manoeuvring”
Viper 31 – “31 ready, 700, balanced”
Viper 32 – “32 ready, same, balanced”
Viper 33 – “33 ready, 600, balanced”
Viper 34 – “34, negative, 700, heavy right 70” (i.e., right tank is 70 lbs more than 
left)
Examples for changing positions and manoeuvring within a formation flight. If done via 
R/T the intra-flight radio should be used:
Echelon Left/Right/LA Ld - “Vipers, Echelon left/right”
Fighting Wing Ld - “Vipers, Fighting Wing”
Route Ld - “Vipers, Route”
Delayed Turns Ld - “Vipers, 90/45 Left/Right”
In Place Turns Ld - “Vipers, In Place 90/45 Left/Right”
Check Turns Ld - “Vipers, Check 20 Left/Right”
Hook Turns Ld – “Vipers, Hook Left/Right”
Shackle Ld - “Vipers, Shackle”
Cross Turn Ld - “Vipers, Cross-turn”
f. Terminate/Knock it Off. At the completion of advanced formation manoeuvring, 
or for other reasons as described in the Training Rules, a terminate call will be 
made. Formation member initiating the terminate will complete the call, with 
justification if able, followed in succession from lowest to highest formation 
number. Terminate can be called by any member of the formation. Any 
TERMINATE or KNOCK IT OFF on either the PRI or AUX radios will use full 
callsigns for all formation members.
Viper 32 – “TERMINATE, TERMINATE, Viper 32 TERMINATE (BINGO)”
Viper 31 – “Viper 31 TERMINATE”
Viper 33 – “Viper 33 TERMINATE”
Viper 34 – “Viper 34 TERMINATE”
NOTE: Knock it off is completed in the same manner as req’d by training rules.
g. Tactical Lead Change. If req’d to change lead aircraft, once the positive role 
change has been established, each member will conduct req’d internal checks and 
the formation as a whole will conduct an Ops check.
Viper 31: “Viper 32, you have tac lead on the left”
Viper 32: “Viper 32, has the tac lead on the left”

104
Viper 32 (new lead): “Vipers, Ops check”
Viper 32: “32, 550, balanced”
Viper 31: “31, same, balanced”
h. Fence In/Out
(1) Lead: “Viper 31, Fence-In”; Wingmen: “Viper 32, Fence-In”, “Viper 33, 
Fence-In” etc.
i. Initiation of Checks. (if required)
(1) Lead: “Vipers, climb and level-off check, push Aux 16“, or “Cowboys, 
pre-descent check, push 3 via ATIS”. Wingmen do not acknowledge and 
wait for check-in on PRI radio as described above.
j. Ancillary Selection. The ancillary is selected by Lead either Up or Down just 
slightly after the transmission is completed with the only delay being the time that 
is required to move the left hand from the PTT button to the respective ancillary.
(1) Lead: “Vipers, Landing gear, Landing gear, Go”;
(2) Lead: “Vipers, Speed brake, Speed brake, go”; and
(3) Lead: “Vipers, Take-off / Landing Flaps, Take-off / Landing Flaps, go”.
k. Power Calls. If power adjustments are required during the formation take-off, the 
wingman must communicate with lead:
(1) Wingman requires lead to add power - Wingman: “Push it up”
(2) Wingman requires lead to reduce power – Wingman: “Give me one”.

105
CHAPTER 9 – NAVIGATION FLYING
2 CFFTS Low Flying Area
1. The 2 CFFTS CT155/CT156 LFA is an area bounded by a line beginning at 49’00”N 
104’00”W to 49’00”N 108’00”W to 53’00”N 108’00”W to 53’00”N 104’00”W thence to the 
point of beginning. 2 CFFTS low-level NAV training shall be conducted within this LFA.
2. Restrictions listed in the RCAF FOM apply and aircrew are responsible for knowing 
them.
3. The following additional restrictions will apply to 2 CFFTS aircrew:
a. all low-level navigation missions must have current sensitive citizens/avoidance 
areas marked on maps;
b. all low-level NAV routes, with the exception of departures and arrivals, will avoid 
the cities of Moose Jaw, Regina, Saskatoon and Swift Current by at least 5 nm or
5000’MSL. All other built-up areas are to be avoided as detailed in higher orders.
Restrictions to Low-level Navigation Training
4. Due to NMAC rates with aircraft involved in aerial application operations, the following 
amendments to low-level navigation training restrictions are in place:
a. A maximum of six Harvards are permitted to fly low-level navigation routes per 
wave, with no more than four Harvards in either direction (North/South).
NOTE: Formations flights are considered one aircraft. 
5. Flight schedulers shall indicate in the “Details” field on each strip whether the routing is 
to the north or south. 
6. Harvard II aircraft shall not be flown lower than 500 ft above obstacles within 500 ft of 
track on approved low-level missions.
7. MDR NAV exercises are to take place within the LFA and shall not be lower than 500 ft 
above all obstacles within 1000 ft of track.
8. Dual NAV training shall not be conducted with weather less than 1500’ AGL cloud base 
and three miles visibility.
9. Solo NAV training shall not be conducted with weather less than 2000’ AGL cloud base 
and five miles visibility.

106
10. TAC NAV shall not be conducted with weather less than 2000’ AGL cloud base and 5 
miles visibility.
Weather Aborts
11. Pilots on Nav exercises encountering marginal weather conditions shall discontinue the 
exercise, return to base, and advise 2 CFFTS Ops O of conditions encountered and suitability for 
other exercises.
Basic Navigation Test (BNT) During Migratory Season
12. When 2 CFFTS is operating on a Heavy Migratory Bird program, the BNT may be 
allowed to proceed, given the following conditions are met:
a. OPS has authorised the conduct of NAV in the area of the mission;
b. The SP has received sufficient training (minimum one mission) to deal with the 
situation of starting the NAV at a minimum altitude of 1000' AGL, and has 
displayed the ability to properly make the decision to lower the minimum altitude 
to 500' AGL as applicable; and
c. 1000' AGL will be treated as a minimum altitude the same way 500' AGL is 
normally, and will be assessed accordingly.
13. It is recommended that the Flights track the amount of time spent at altitudes other than 
500' AGL to ensure sufficient training was offered the SP to develop their 500' sight picture. 
Flight Plans / Itineraries
14. Prior to departure the aircrew will contact AIS Flight Planning and provide the required 
information as depicted in the 2 CFFTS Gen Book; or, IAW an ICAO Flight Plan. 
15. Students will file VFR Flight Plans for any low-level Nav test or Solo missions.
16. Aircrew shall leave a copy of the intended NAV route map, annotated with date, call 
sign, and sign out time in the Nav Deconfliction folder, a minimum of one-hour prior to the start 
of the wave in question.
Departure for Low-level Nav Routes
17. When departing on low-level missions, all aircraft will normally climb on the extended 
runway centreline to at least 4000 ft ASL. Overflight of the traffic patterns should be avoided.
Low-level NAV (LLN) R/T 
 

107
18. When rejoining the Traffic Pattern in a manner that is less commonly used, or a 
significant time has elapsed from the initial call to Tower and arriving at the initial while 
returning from the North on an LLN, the following RT shall be used:
 
a. Rejoining/Entering Traffic Pattern from the downwind (CH 2/3)
 
(1) Pilot "Apollo 12, entering the pattern from the downwind"
