(2) Tower "Apollo 12, Roger," or "Apollo 12, watch for traffic turning 
crosswind"; and
(3) Pilot "Apollo 12"
 
b. Rejoining/Entering Traffic Pattern from base leg (after time interval from initial 
call to tower) (CH 2 Only)
 
(1) Pilot "Apollo 12, entering the pattern from base leg"
(2) Tower "Apollo 12, roger," or "Apollo 12, watch for traffic on the 
downwind";
(3) Pilot "Apollo 12".
Navigation Routes and Standardization
19. All 2 CFFTS NAV routes are the sole property of 2 CFFTS. It is strictly prohibited for 
any undergraduate pilot to fly a 2 CFFTS NAV route or portion thereof in aircraft other than 
those belonging to 2 CFFTS. It is equally prohibited for undergraduate pilots to fly Standards 
Navigation Routes unless on a NAV test.
20. The intent of standardizing the Navigation routes is to assist in deconflicting of Phase II 
Navigation and Phase III Tac Nav trips. This has been accomplished by not reusing any 
Hacks/Turn Points/Targets, minimal crossings, depiction of crossing routes, and maximum use 
of the low level flying area.
21. Phase III Harvard routes will still require deconfliction. As such all pilots flying 
navigation trips will still be required to conduct a detailed map study of other routes being flown 
during their launch window to identify crossing points.
22. Flight schedulers will be required to identify the Nav route being flown through the use 
of a “details” field in Flight Pro a minimum of 1 hour prior to takeoff. In the case of a nonStandardized route, the direction of the route will be included i.e. Nav 3N, Nav 2S, or Nav N or 
Nav S.
23. Non-standard route maps must added to the Nav Deconfliction folder on the drive a 
minimum of 1 hour prior to takeoff to ensure maximum deconfliction amongst all pilots flying 

108
that wave and to allow time for routes to be entered into the ops desk computer prior to the start 
of the wave. 
24. Due to the increased number of crossings, Nav 1 North should only be used when 
southern weather is considered questionable. Additionally, routes titled ED shall only be used for 
extra dual trips or staff training (on a limited basis).
25. Further distinction is made about routes according to the following guidelines:
a. Block A routes (pre-solo) are South/North A1, North A2, North A3, South A2;
b. Solo route is South Solo;
c. Block B routes (post Solo) are North B2, North B3, North B4, South B1 an South 
B2, and
d. Routes can be flown in any order within the Block except South/North A1 that is 
dedicated to the Nav1 sortie.''
26. The maximum number of Low Level Nav Routes per wave will remain at 6 Harvards 
Deviation from this will only occur on a case by case basis depending on the schedule (i.e. 4 
Harvards, etc). This limit may be reduced or eliminated in the future.
27. To eliminate time and fuel being wasted, waypoints for Tac Nav routes will be preloaded into all Harvard GPS units. Allocation will be as follows: FP21 (Tac Nav 1), FP22 (Tac 
Nav 2), and FP23 (Tac Nav 3). These flight plan slots are not to be used for any other trips than 
Tac Nav.
28. During navigation flights in uncontrolled airspace, pilots shall continuously monitor 
126.7 MHz and whenever practical, broadcast their identification, position, altitude and 
intentions.
29. This standardization will help mitigate the risk of low level NMACs, however this does 
not take the place of proper route deconfliction, effective lookout, and clear position reports 
using major features.
Navigation Test Routes
30. Standards will control all NAV test routes. The route will be assigned by Standards 
following the successful completion of the NAV pre-test. It will be considered acceptable to fly 
the route on the same day as it was assigned provided the student has, as a minimum, four hours 
to plan after receiving the route. At no time are these routes to be flown as syllabus Review or 
ED missions without prior coordination with Standards.

109
31. In the event there is a requirement for a Review trip prior to the NAV test, the originally 
assigned NAV Test route will be returned to Standards prior to the review trip. Standards will 
assign a different NAV route following the Review mission.
Low-level Traffic Deconfliction Plan
32. While many aspects of the low-level deconfliction plan remain the same, several changes 
have been implemented to further improve the overall safety of flight without compromising the 
flexibility of operations. The rationale for these changes is to avoid, to the maximum extent 
possible, NMACs between NFTC aircraft while operating in the low level environment.
33. As the next phase in strengthening a traffic deconfliction culture, 2 CFFTS will 
implement a program aimed at improving aircrew situational awareness and communication 
among pilots in the low level environment. These procedures will also serve to enhance flight 
safety by fostering an improved lookout and traffic deconfliction mindset in all aircrew.
34. It is not the intention of this section to establish rigid restrictions, but rather to simply 
improve lookout and mitigate the potential for traffic conflicts in the low level environment 
inside and outside of the Moose Jaw MTCA. 
35. Deconfliction planning with known traffic shall be incorporated into the part of pre-flight 
brief. Altitude separation shall be incorporated as a primary means of deconfliction at 
appropriate points due to inherent limitations of human vision especially when operating in the 
low level environment.
36. Flights shall strive to adhere to takeoff times, but realizing due to a variety of reasons 
often outside their control this is not always possible- caution should be applied to estimating 
airborne conflicts with other Nav trips especially PH III NAV trips due to the ramp time required 
to manually input waypoints. Further, as diversions are a part of PH III syllabus, such diversions 
should be planned in advance by the Aircraft Captain and ideally coordinated in advance with 
other potentially conflicting Aircraft.
Look-out
37. All QFIs are to ensure that more emphasis is placed on teaching the importance and the 
method of executing a more effective lookout in the Low Level environment. QFIs are directed 
to make specific mention of the quality and/or frequency of lookout performed by the student, on 
any or all progress cards where appropriate, in an attempt to increase overall awareness. In 
addition, the UFSO and Flight FSOs will provide regular flight safety awareness briefings to all 
aircrew with specific emphasis on lookout included in these briefings. 
38. Harvard aircrew performing low level tactical navigation profiles shall turn their anticollision lights on as soon as practicable when not in close formation and operating within the 
low level environment. This measure will likely increase the formation's visibility for other 
traffic operating the in the vicinity during low light conditions.

110
39. Harvard low-level sorties will be flown only using aircraft equipped with a functioning 
TAS.
Hack Delay Procedures
40. With a limited number of NAV routes available, it is not uncommon to have more than 
one aircraft flying identical routes on the same launch wave. In this instance, it is incumbent on 
the pilots to ensure there is a plan to de-conflict at the Hack if a delay is required by the first 
aircraft. 
a. To mitigate any further conflicts at low-level Hacks, if an aircraft requires a reHack or loiter at the planned Hack, aircrew will broadcast their intentions on VHF 
CH 20 (126.7).
Map Checking
41. Prior to flying a low level navigation mission, pilots shall cross reference other 
navigation maps located in the maps folder on the drive. The Duty OpsO will provide some 
assistance to raise the pilot's awareness of potential conflicting traffic; however, the 
responsibility of ensuring adequate steps have been taken to mitigate traffic conflicts ALWAYS 
rest with the PIC. In the event that aircrew become aware of a potential traffic conflict with 
another aircraft that has already departed, the onus will rest with the last crew to walk to actively 
deconflict as soon as practicable once airborne.
Broadcasting of Position Reports
42. Pilots operating VFR in and outside of the MTCA in uncontrolled airspace shall 
continuously monitor 126.7 MHz and whenever practical, broadcast their identification, position, 
altitude and intentions.
43. Position reports shall conform to the following general format: "Avonlea area traffic this 
is Snapper 12 10 NM west of Avonlea heading southeast at 500' AGL. Estimating Radville area 
in 7 minutes. Snapper 12."
44. As crop dusting flights are not typically familiar with area lakes, the standard position 
reports should refer primarily to significant towns/cities/aerodromes for North and South flights 
such as:
NORTH SOUTH
Moose Jaw Moose Jaw
Regina Gravelbourg
Saskatoon Assiniboia
Regina Beach Avonlea
Manitou Beach/Watrous Rockglen
Craik Swift Current
Davidson Radville

111
Swift Current Mankota
Fort Qu' Appelle Regina
Lucky Lake Pangman
45. Notwithstanding the standardized "broadcast format," if there are potential conflicts 
realized when airborne, nothing prevents further commonsense R/T being used to ensure 
deconfliction including reference to major lakes or other significant features. 
Mental Dead Reckoning (MDR)
46. Planning of a low level MDR should be normally completed at 1000' AGL or higher. 
This will also ensure appropriate time is available to conduct proper lookout techniques.
Low-level Nav Chase Plane Procedures 
Refs: A. 2 CFFTS Orders, Book 2, Chapter 3 Hot/Cold Weather Operations
47. For the purpose of completing low-level NAV missions when single-ship low-level ops 
are not permitted IAW ref A, the following procedures shall be adhered to. The chase aircraft is 
provided in a support role to ensure that the appropriate agencies are immediately advised in the 
event of an ejection by the low-level aircraft, and to provide the initial search low function until 
relieved. Due to proximity of the aircraft and the chase plane’s requirement to manoeuvre in 
relation to the low-level aircraft when utilizing these procedures, the aircraft shall be considered 
a Formation.
48. Crew Requirements. 
a. Harvard: At least one Element Lead is required between the two aircraft. The 
chase aircraft requires a QFI holding a minimum B category of instruction.
49. Responsibilities. Each aircrew will brief and adhere to the low-level Training Rules as 
outlined in the SMM. Each aircrew will have a copy of the route map complete with any 
obstacles and planned deviations.
50. Briefing. The mission shall be briefed by a QFI in the lead aircraft as a Formation, and 
include the following as a minimum:
a. routing;
b. altitudes and spacing;
c. communication procedures/frequencies;
d. weather abort considerations;
e. altitude separation plan in the event of inadvertent IMC;

112
f. emergency considerations;
g. RTB plan or the location of the formation split; and,
h. revisionary NAV requirements or deviations if applicable.
51. Weather Requirements. The minimum weather shall be a ceiling of 2000 ft AGL, 5 miles 
visibility, and a discernable horizon. 
52. R/T. The low-level NAV aircraft should obtain taxi clearance for both aircraft. The lead 
aircraft QFI shall be responsible for any formation related R/T. Recommended radio 
management: inter-plane formation frequency shall be set on UHF and 126.7 shall be set on the 
VHF. For HVD aircraft, OPS or other frequencies deemed necessary should be monitored on the 
secondary VHF frequency. To ensure that a comms failure doesn’t go undetected, chase aircraft 
will respond to lead aircraft’s position reports: “Demon 11 position report 126.7……….”,
Response: “12”. If no position report is made, lead aircraft shall initiate a standard check-in once 
per leg: “Demon 11 Flight right/left”, Response “12”
53. Mission Conduct.
a. The aircraft will start, taxi as a Formation and conduct the take-off as briefed by 
the Formation Lead (normally interval).
b. Harvard chase aircraft shall fly at a minimum of 1000’AGL, and TAC Nav 
minima apply.
c. The chase aircraft should keep the low-level aircraft below the horizon terrain, 
conditions permitting.
d. To minimize interference with single-ship training, the chase aircraft will remain 
no closer than Fighting Wing or as briefed by the formation Lead. 
e. Close-Formation rejoins shall not be conducted in the low-level environment.
f. The chase aircraft will navigate the route to ensure safe separation from all enroute obstacles.
54. Mission Abort. If the low-level portion of the mission is aborted, both aircraft shall climb 
out of the low-level environment, de-conflict on the inter-Formation frequency and obtain ATC 
clearances if required. The mission shall be aborted for any of the following reasons:
a. an emergency situation develops in either aircraft;
b. either aircraft is unable to maintain safe separation from obstacles or weather 
limits;

113
c. the aircraft are not able to maintain radio communication for any reason; 
d. the chase aircraft cannot maintain/regain visual with the low-level aircraft.
55. Lost lead. In the event of lost lead with the low-level aircraft in VMC or IMC conditions, 
the following procedures shall be followed:
a. VMC. The chase aircraft shall climb to a minimum of 1500 ft AGL and the interformation frequency should be used to resolve the situation. Once the low-level 
aircraft is visually acquired, the chase aircraft can descend to previous alt.
b. IMC. The chase aircraft shall carry out the standard IMC Lost Wingman 
Procedure, communicate altitude separation and obtain an ATC clearance.
56. Flight Plan. A single flight plan shall be filed to include the low-level aircraft and the 
chase aircraft.

114
CHAPTER 10 - EMERGENCIES
Abnormal Flying Characteristics
1. Aircraft displaying abnormal flying characteristics (e.g. flick roll, excessive wing drop, 
spin abnormalities) are to be placed unserviceable upon landing. Consideration will be given to 
conducting a controllability check IAW aircraft AFM/AOIs.
Unsafe Landing Gear
2. A visual check by another aircraft or the Tower should be used to confirm that the gear 
appears to be down when it is not indicating down and locked.
3. When a landing has been made with unsafe gear down indications, the aircraft should be 
stopped on the runway for installation of the landing gear pins. The Harvard must be shutdown 
prior to installation of the landing gear pins.
4. Under no circumstances are touch and go landings to be executed with an unserviceable 
landing gear system.
Harvard Nose Wheel Alignment
5. Harvard pilots are reminded that the potential exists for the nose wheel to become 
misaligned post-take-off or following a touch and go. Nose wheel misalignment greater than 
approximately 45 degrees will inhibit the aircraft from being able to self-centre the wheel during 
the retraction cycle. This may result in an unsafe up indication of the nose wheel due to 
interference with the gear doors on retraction. In all cases to date, subsequent down selection of 
the gear resulted in the gear properly cycling to the "down and locked" position with the correct 
3 green indication, allowing for a successful recovery. Past instances of landing with a 
misaligned nose wheel resulted in the aircraft veering left or right.
6. The following procedure is to be followed if a nose wheel unsafe up condition is 
encountered in flight:
a. When conditions permit, select the landing gear "down" and follow the "Landing 
Gear Malfunction" section in the yellow pages of the checklist.
b. Attempt to determine if the nose wheel is properly aligned by having another 
aircraft conduct a visual inspection or by flying by the control tower and having 
them conduct a visual inspection; and
c. If any degree of nose wheel misalignment is confirmed, DO NOT CYCLE the 
landing gear.
7. If the nose wheel is confirmed to be misaligned, proceed as follows:

115
a. Land on the main wheels and hold the nose wheel off of the runway;
b. When safely able, momentarily engage and release nose wheel steering; and
c. Slowly lower the nose wheel to the runway. If directional problems occur, engage 
nose wheel steering and maintain aircraft control. If runway departure is 
imminent, carry out the "Abort" procedure.
8. Pilots flying in formation should ensure that both aircraft are configured in a safe 
condition for landing. A visual inspection of the landing gear of each aircraft should be made to 
confirm down and locked and that the nose wheel is aligned correctly.
Harvard Rudder Restriction/Lock
9. In 2008, there were several incidents involving CT156 rudder restrictions/lock during 
flight. Although investigated thoroughly, the cause of these incidents has yet to be determined.
10. Recently, the USAF experienced several similar incidents. The symptoms of the rudder 
restrictions were stiff rudders in the centre position until touchdown, when the rudders then 
became fully functional again.
11. Two of the recent USAF incident pilots were able to return the rudder to normal 
operation by selecting the PRESSURIZATION SWITCH to RAM/DUMP. In one of the 
incidents, the aircrew restored the cabin pressure and the rudder subsequently locked again. The 
aircrew then reselected RAM/DUMP and the rudder returned to normal operation; the 
occurrence was also partially replicated during ground testing.
12. The exact mechanics of why this action rectified the rudder issue is not yet fully 
understood and to date the RCAF has yet to receive any recommendations from the Original 
Equipment Manufacturer (OEM). Although no direction has been given by the OEM regarding 
the selection of RAM/DUMP to solve a rudder restriction/lock issue, the purpose of this AIF is 
to inform aircrew that this action may rectify the problem if rudder restriction/lock is 
experienced during flight.
13. If aircrew experience a rudder restriction/ lock in flight and decide to select RAM/DUMP 
on the pressurization switch in attempt to rectify the problem, it is recommended that it be done 
below 8000'MSL and to leave the switch in the RAM/DUMP position until the landing is 
completed. 
14. If the aircrew are landing at an airport that is greater than 8000' elevation and/or elects to 
select RAM/DUMP above 8000' MSL, aircrew are recommended to make the selection prior to 
commencing the approach or entering the Traffic Pattern. Crews are also reminded that they may 
feel the effects of rapid decompression when selecting RAM/DUMP. 
Loss of Rudder and Brake Control 

116
15. The intent of this section is to describe the following incident and what to expect with 
respect to handling conditions should a pilot experience this failure.
16. On 18 April 2012, the aircrew of a CT-156 experienced what they described as a loss of 
right brake and directional control in the rear cockpit on takeoff. Control was passed to the front 
seat pilot, and the aircraft was able to regain control and recovered without further incident.
17. Investigation revealed a retaining clip was missing from the inboard end of the rudder 
pedal pivot shaft in the right rear seat rudder pedal that allowed the shaft to become disengaged 
on the inboard side. This shaft is attached to the aircraft structure on the outboard side by a bolt 
and unlikely that the shaft could become completely disengaged with the inboard side loose.
18. Ground testing by maintenance crew revealed that if aircrew were to experience this 
failure they could expect to still have some rudder control and brake authority from the affected 
seat position, however, the rudder pedal action will feel different then what is normally expected.
The pilot can expect to feel excessive movement in both rudder and brake control beyond normal 
range, similar to a spongy sensation due to the torque moment caused by the rudder being 
secured on only the outboard side. 
Speed Brake Droop with Emergency Landing Gear Extension
19. The manufacturer has confirmed the possibility of speed brake droop following an 
emergency landing gear extension. It is caused by reduced pressure in the speed brake 
accumulator, and there may be buffeting and additional drag to overcome. It is possible that an
additional 10-15% torque is required to overcome the extra drag. Once the emergency extension 
handle has been operated the speed brake cannot be operated (retracted or extended). The droop 
will not be significant enough to interfere with ground clearance on landing.
Airsickness Report Form
20. This section provides guidance on the use of the Student Airsickness Report found in the 
Aircrew Reference. The intent of the form is to ensure that MIR clinical staff are receiving 
accurate information regarding the effects of airsickness on students, their missions, and their 
course progress. Without accurate reporting, clinicians are unable to provide appropriate tools 
and treatments to students, and unable to provide reliable assessments to the training system.
21. The Student Airsickness Form included in Annex U is to be completed when a SP/CM is 
actively airsick; a msn is classified as DNCO or DPCO due to airsickness; or, at the QFI's 
discretion.
22. The QFI is to complete questions 1 through 5.
23. If course progress is affected, the respective Flt Comd should provide a comment in 
question 6. Impact to course progress would normally imply that the SP / CM's airsickness is not 
improving; treatments/trg from MIR have not been effective; and, the flight is unable to progress 
the SP / CM normally due to requirements for additional RE missions.

117
24. When a Student Airsickness Form is completed, the SP / CM will hand carry the form to 
the MIR. The SP / CM shall report to MIR as soon as is practical for assessment, normally the 
same day (however, if the SP / CM cannot arrive at the MIR prior to 1500 hrs, they shall report 
the next morning at 0730 hrs).
25. Once reviewed and signed by the clinician, the SP / CM is to return the form, along with 
a chit to their Flt Comd. A copy is to be placed on the SP / CM's trg file.
Ground Emergencies
26. Aircrew should be aware of the crash response services available and although immediate 
threat to their aircraft may be secure, should consider available resources in mitigating follow on 
safety on the airfield.
27. Aircrew shall declare an emergency on the ground in the following situations: (1) 
potential FOD on runway; (2) an explosive device has been detonated (such as the canopy 
fracture); (3) potential injury to crew members or ground crew; and (4) any situation the crew 
deem emergency response is appropriate.
28. The Duty Ops O may declare an emergency through Tower if they witness an event 
where the crew members may be unable to declare the emergency themselves (for example if 
they are emergency egressing).
Flight Safety Reporting
29. Any time an emergency is declared, or a Flight Safety occurrence takes place, a Flight 
Safety incident shall be reported. Aircrew are reminded to inform the ground crew as soon as 
possible so that the aircraft can be quarantined, rather than refueled, so that the appropriate Flight 
Safety initial actions are not compromised. 
30. If there is any doubt as to whether a Flight Safety occurrence has taken place, the 
occurrence shall be reported as an incident. Procedures published by the UFSO are to be 
followed.
Ground Egress Training in Aircraft
31. Emergency ground egress training is not to be done in the Harvard aircraft. Emergency 
ground egress training is to be confined to the ejection trainers.

118
CHAPTER 11 - AIR DISPLAYS
Air Displays - General
1. Air displays shall be conducted IAW the RCAF FOM and B-GA-100-003/AA-000.
Conduct of Static Display Taskings
2. Responsibilities of Mission Commander (Aircraft Captain) tasked with the fulfilment of a 
static display tasking is responsible for the following:
a. to ensure that pilots tasked with air show static display duties are on duty at the 
aircraft as stipulated by the Air show Director to the maximum extent possible. 
b. To ensure pilots are suitably attired in 2 CFFTS approved flying clothing.
c. to make arrangements with air show organisers to provide access stairs or 
platforms to facilitate supervised viewing of the cockpit by the public;
d. to ensure that the aircraft to be displayed is in presentable condition;
e. subject to availability, to provide complimentary lapel badges to members of the 
public visiting the static display; and,
f. provide a post-ex report to 2 CFFTS Ops and A3 Spec Events as directed by the 
airshow tasking message.
Formation and Solo Flying Display 
3. 2 CFFTS will provide aircraft that will follow 1 Cdn Air Div directions and procedures. 
Altitude and other restrictions in CFAO 5511 and BGA100003/AA000 shall be adhered to.
4. The Cmdt will designate Formation Comds and Solo pilots authorised to conduct air 
displays other than flypasts. The Formation Comd or Solo pilot is to brief the CFI at least 48 hrs 
in advance.
5. An approved Standardized Solo Harvard CT156 Air Demonstration has been approved 
by 1 and 2 Cdn Air Div and is provided at Annex I. It will only be performed by those authorized 
by the Cmdt to do so. To be considered for approval the applicant should have as a minimum: (1)
previous air demonstration experience (e.g. Snowbirds, Hornet demo pilot); or (2) 
Sufficient CT156 flying experience.
6. Once approval from Cmdt is received, the member will undergo training and a checkout 
flight with the current Air Demonstration OPI prior to being authorized to perform the 
demonstration for an airshow performance. 

119
CHAPTER 12 – TEMPORARY DUTY/CROSS-COUNTRY PROCEDURES/CLAIMS 
Refs: A. Canadian Forces Temporary Duty Instruction (CFTDI)
B. RCAF FOM
General
1. All 2 CFFTS pilots planning overnight missions are to submit a CrossCountry Trip 
Request (TR) which shall include all enroute stops. The Trip Request can be found digitally on 
JIRA (same login as Aircrew Reference Library). All refueling stops must be shown. Changes to 
refueling stops can be changed as needed during the trip. However, any changes in RON to the 
original TR after submission must go through the Duty Ops O. Any significant changes prior to 
departure will require a new TR submission. When selecting destination and booking hotels, 
aircrew should familiarize themselves with the above reference to ensure their trip is IAW 2 
CFFTS policy. The farthest point on a cross country must normally lie inside a 1200 nm radius 
from Moose Jaw. Exceptions to this rule may be granted on a case by case basis.
2. An Individual Travel Authorization (ITA) form and the approved TR shall be submitted 
for signature NLT 48 hours prior to planned departure. This form can be obtained through the 
Aircrew Reference SharePoint or Defence Forms Catalogue on DWAN, form 2999.
3. Before submitting the TR, the Aircraft Captain shall ensure that it is complete and 
correct. In particular, checking it for appropriate airfield facilities, PPR numbers, airfield 
operating times, sufficient crew rest periods etc. TRs to Canadian destinations and U.S. civilian 
destinations must be submitted to Ops Flt no later than 1300L the Monday prior to departure. 
TRs to U.S. military destinations must be submitted to Ops no later than 14 days prior to the 
departure date. Out-and-Backs must be received by Ops by 1300L on day prior to departure. 
Out-and-Backs with the intent to conduct night training or additional flights must be submitted 
one week prior to departure in order to afford maintenance sufficient time to prep aircraft with 
the required hours available.
4. Rental cars shall be authorized by the Admin O using a cost comparison. A list of preapproved rental destinations can be found with the AdminO. NOTE: Pilots must stay within 
100km of the aircraft at all times.
5. It is the member’s responsibility to ensure the hotel rate is within the city rate limit as per 
the Public Service and Procurement Canada (PSPC) directive. Note that a hotel in the directory 
with a higher rate than the published maximum city rate will only be reimbursed at said City rate. 
Deviation from this rate shall be requested before the charge is incurred by the ITA approver. 
6. The requestor’s supervisor shall examine the request to ensure it is correct. The 
supervisor must concur with the request and be satisfied that the itinerary is acceptable from the 
viewpoint of staff availability, timing, airfield facilities and pilot capabilities. The supervisor of 
shall then assess the crew's requirement for the mission, verify no crew currencies will expire 
within 14 days of the planned departure date, ensure leave passes are attached if required IAW 
10.04(1) below, digitally sign the TR and forward it to the 2 CFFTS Ops O.

120
7. Any VFR flight during cross-countries must be briefed and approved by the Cmdt/CFI 
prior to departure via the CoC. This does not restrict the ability to cancel IFR while on approach 
and proceed directly to the destination via a visual approach, nor from taking off VFR on course 
in order to pickup an IFR clearance airborne at the point of departure. It also does not preclude 
day VFR pattern work carried out at destination aerodrome.
8. Only those trip requests reviewed by the 2 CFFTS Admin O and approved by the 2 
CFFTS OpsO will be forwarded to the Cmdt for approval. The 2 CFFTS Ops O shall coordinate 
with CAE to ensure aircraft are available subsequent to mission approval.
9. A Trip Request and claim are only required for Out-and-Backs when staying overnight at 
destination. An MTEC can be used for same day trip meals. If an out and back is conducted over 
a weekend, all crew members must be on leave for the day they are not flying. No TD expenses 
can be claimed while on leave.
10. Copies of the original TR can be found in JIRA during and following the trip.
Destinations
11. The landing runway length will not be less than 3000’ for the Harvard II. In any case, 2 
CFFTS aircrew are to ensure the runway length is sufficient based on determination of the 
critical field length as per aircraft specific AOIs. The minimum rwy length requirement may only 
be waived by the Cmdt.
12. Many U.S. and Canadian destinations require a PPR number. If required, these numbers 
must be shown on the trip request when initially submitted.
Delays/Changes/Serviceability Issues
13. All itinerary changes due to weather while on the road are approved provided that the 
new fuel stop/RON meets all of the following criteria:
a. the airfield has a suitable runway and IFR approach;
b. the airfield has suitable arrival/ATC services; and
c. the airfield has the appropriate services.
14. Diversions in the U.S. should, whenever possible, be kept to civilian airfields to allow for 
an easier recovery in the event of an unserviceability, however, recovery at military airfields is 
also approved. In all cases safety shall be the deciding factor.
15. All delayed departures/cancellations and changes in crew, RON or destination shall be 
communicated to the 2 CFFTS Ops O. Hotel rate limits shall be reviewed and authorized at the 
same time, prior to incurring charges. Every attempt should be made to cancel prior hotel 

121
booking stating military and operational reason. If the hotel does not perform the cancellation at 
no cost, hotel name and details should be provided to the Admin O upon return. 
16. If an unserviceability occurs while on the road, the aircraft commander shall inform the 
Duty Ops O and Maintenance Team Lead of the nature of the unserviceability so that remedial 
action can be taken ASAP. A callout sheet is located in the front of each aircraft book.
17. If a flight safety incident occurs, the aircraft captain shall contact the UFSO or delegate 
ASAP. The UFSO’s cell number is located in the front of the aircraft book.
Mutual Cross Country/Out-and-Back
18. Quarterly Cross-Country Proficiency Flights. 2 CFFTS QFIs may be authorised to deploy 
on at least one mutual cross-country msn every quarter. TD funds will be available for crosscountries such that QFIs are authorized one day off at full TD per cross-country. Any requests 
for more than one day without flying while on cross-country trg must include a leave pass for the 
extra day(s) off. TD will not normally be authorised for the extra day(s) (exceptions at the 
Cmdt’s discretion, such as if a recovery window is not available on the Sunday). The Cmdt is the 
approving authority for all cross-country msns. QFIs are responsible for their own trg, and as 
such must make every effort to maximize trg value of staff cross-countries. The purpose of crosscountry proficiency flying is to gain and maintain flying proficiency; the purpose is not to 
provide a paid vacation for CF members. QFIs who fly cross-country proficiency missions shall 
make maximum use of the training opportunity provided. On a normal weekend, QFIs are 
expected to depart Moose Jaw, when able, on the last working day of the week. They may park 
the aircraft for one day or fly; the choice is left at the discretion of the QFIs. Cross-country 
proficiency flights shall either return to Moose Jaw on the Sun (if a Sun arrival window is in 
effect) or place the aircraft within one short hop of Moose Jaw to return for the first wave of 
weekday flying. Ideally, all legs except the last one to Moose Jaw on the first morning of the 
week will include an approach. In the case of long weekends, QFIs are expected to fly every day 
but one – if they plan to park the aircraft for more than one day, weekend leave must be used and 
TD will be halted for the associated period.
19. Out-and-Backs. In addition to the cross-country allocation per QFI, staff are also entitled 
to two Out-and-Backs per fiscal year IAW RCAF FOM. Out-and-Back msns will normally be no 
more than one leg away from Moose Jaw, should not exceed 600 nm. QFIs shall be reimbursed 
for a maximum of three meals and one night’s accommodations. Nominally, out and back 
proficiency flights shall be approved twice per year; however, to maintain flexibility the Cmdt 
may approve additional missions.
20. Mutual cross-country missions will normally depart during last wave. The Ops O may
authorize an earlier departure if the weather is such that the departure will not negatively impact 
student trips. Individual Flt Comds may delay these departures for operational reasons. Earlier 
departures must be approved by the 2 CFFTS Ops O via the Chain of Command. Arrivals will be 
planned for the designated recovery period (normally 1600-1800L on Sunday). In all cases, 
cross-country departures and arrivals shall be planned to minimize impact on the flying 
operation. The AC is personally responsible for all customs procedures outlined in FLIPS. 

122
Requests for departure/returns to base outside approved timings will be substantiated in the 
remarks section of the TR.
21. Approved GPH 204 call signs may be used for cross-countries within Canada. Flights to 
the U.S. will be given a CanForce callsign by 2 CFFTS Ops. Local formation callsigns (e.g. 
Venoms, Cowboys, etc) are not authorized. Cross-country formations shall use GPH 204 call 
signs, e.g. “Cobra-31 Flt”.
Harvard Student Cross-Country
22. Ph III student cross-country missions shall be authorised using normal cross-country 
authorization procedures, with one mission normally being conducted in Canada and one in the 
United States. Flt Comds shall ensure that QFIs with limited cross-country experience conduct a 
mutual cross-country with a more senior QFI prior to being assigned a student cross-country 
mission. Students on Ph III training shall fly cross-country sorties from the front seat.
23. When scheduling student cross-countries, plan to depart no earlier than the third wave on 
Friday and RTB 30 minutes prior to the end of first wave on Monday. This is to ensure that 
Maintenix entries can be completed in time for aircraft to fly in the 2nd wave. The intent is to 
conduct an average of two sorties per day. Any additional trips such as NT or CM must be fitted 
within these timings. It is expected that the aircrew will be flying every day. Mid-week 
departures will not normally be supported. 
Cross-Countries to the U.S.
24. Harvard aircraft are required to arrive in Canada and clear Customs on Sunday if the 
aircraft is to arrive in the first wave Monday morning. The Ops O may approve exceptions. 
25. Each aircraft binder will contain a copy of the annual RCAF aircraft diplomatic flight 
clearance letter from the US Department of State. This document shall be presented if requested 
by US Customs Officers.
Pre-Flight Procedures
26. On receipt of an approved Cross-Country Request Authorization, the aircraft captain shall 
note any special instructions or restrictions and comply with them.
27. Aircraft captains shall:
a. report to Ops on the day of the cross country prior to leaving. Crew names should 
already be poste on the Big 2 Ops Board. Crews will add their phone numbers to 
the board next to their names. Crews will text the Duty Ops O with one of the 
crew member’s names prior to leaving ops to establish a line of communication.
b. obtain a copy of the TR. This copy is the authorization to travel.

123
c. sign out any required kit (pubs bag, cell phone, tablet, etc.) and write down the kit 
numbers on the Big 2 Ops Board.
d. obtain a flyaway kit if applicable to aircraft type at CMAT desk, including 
relevant credit cards. The preferred order of use is: (1) CAE issued credit card 
(MasterCard); and (2) ARI Fleet card.
e. sign-out in FlightPro;
KLN900 Data Base Limitations and Procedures
28. The KLN900 GPS data base packages have been divided into three regions:
a. Americas Canada: provides detailed airport information in all Canada and into the 
USA as far south as OR, ID, WY, KS, MO, KY, and VA;
b. Americas South: provides detailed airport information in all Mexico and into the 
USA as far north as CA, NV, UT, CO, OK, AK, TN, and NC; and
c. Americas USA will cover the lower 48 states only.
29. The Harvard II KLN900 GPS is programmed with the Americas Canada data base 
only. Reduced GPS capability and information available will be noticed by crews travelling to 
the southern U.S. These GPS limitations include: Limited airport information (Lat/Long and 
elevation only); No RNAV STAR, SID, or Approach data; and, No intersection data.
30. Harvard II crews travelling to the affected areas will not be able to perform RNAV(GPS) 
or GPS approaches, RNAV STARS, or RNAV SIDS unless the database is uploaded. The 
database for the continental United States is available and should be issued to crews travelling to 
the United States. Airport and VOR lat & long information is still available in the database for 
enroute navigation.
31. If the crew elects to not upload the database for the affected area, then when 
completing/filing US domestic flight plans (FAA Form 7233-1) for the affected area, 
information block three shall be completed as follows:
a. Flight contained within Americas Canada data base – TEX2/G (Global 
Navigation Systems incl. GPS or WAAS with enroute & terminal capability);
b. Flight outside Americas Canada data base – TEX2/I (VOR/DME with 
Transponder Mode C).
32. Areas south of the highlighted line are affected by the KLN900 GPS data base changes:

124
Departure/Arrival Messages
33. A Departure Message is required for the first departure of the day, and any significant 
changes (Serviceability, change of Enroute stop…etc.).
34. An Arrival Message is required for the arrival at the last destination, and any significant 
changes (Serviceability, change of RON…etc.).
35. The Departure /Arrival text format shall include the following information: 
a. Callsign:
b. A/C Comd:
c. Departed/Arrived & Loc:
d. Aircraft state:
e. Remarks:
36. These messages can be send via sign out phones and/or personal phones whichever the 
PIC decides and shall be sent to 2 CFFTS OPS Personnel and to their respective Flt Comds. A 
reference card has been created and is located in the A/C binders. 
Enroute Stop Procedures
37. 2 CFFTS crews should use the listed Military Contract (MILCON) fuel service provider 
found in the CAF Flight Supplement (GPH205(S)) for all refueling. If no contracted provider is 
available or listed, alternative providers may be used. For en-route stops in the USA, crews 
should utilize a service provider that, in addition to fuel, can provide additional services such as 
ground power units and/or hangar space in the event that they are required.

125
38. If aircraft must be left unattended even for a short time, the canopy shall be closed and 
the seat and canopy safety pins shall be installed. Harvard II aircrew must abide by flight manual 
limitations with regard to equipment cooling limitations with ambient temperatures of 35C and 
above.
39. A qualified crewmember will assist with and supervise all aircraft turnarounds.
WARNING: Due to explosive devices, the cockpit area is out of bounds to unauthorised 
personnel.
40. The AC shall ensure that the instructions contained in the Travelling Kit regarding 
Servicing Checks are followed.
41. When the aircraft is to be left overnight, safety pins and covers shall be installed. During 
the winter months, consideration should be given to securing hangar space to minimize wing 
contamination due to frost. If so equipped, the canopy and luggage compartment shall be locked. 
Pilots are to ensure a contact phone number is left with the Servicing Agent.
42. The use of JP8 with +100 additive is prohibited. In the event of a diversion where JP8 
with +100 is the only fuel available, specific authorization from the Duty Crew Chief must be 
obtained.
43. All fuel receipts/vouchers shall be retained (preferably stapled to appropriate green sheet) 
ensuring they are correctly completed to legibly include pilot rank, name and unit.
44. Use of Glycol/De-icing fluid is prohibited on all NFTC aircraft.
45. When operating aircraft away from Moose Jaw, care must be taken to ensure proper fuel 
with icing inhibitors is used as per the AOI/CFACM. The following shall be adhered to for 
Harvard aircraft:
a. If the characteristics of the fuel are known and a fuel spec sheet is available from 
the refuelling contractor, determine the freezing point and the viscosity at -20°C 
from the spec sheet:
(1) if the viscosity at -20°C is 3.00 to 3.99 then the limit is -49°C;
(2) if the viscosity at -20°C is 4.00 to 4.99 then the limit is -42°C;
(3) if the viscosity at -20°C is 5.00 to 5.99 then the limit is -38°C; or
(4) if the viscosity is 6.00 or above or not shown then the limit is -29°C.
b. if the characteristics of the fuel are not know then the temperature limit for all 
operations away from Moose Jaw is -29°C; and

126
c. crews shall obtain the necessary information to determine an appropriate cruising 
altitude to ensure that they abide by the temperature limits in 8.a. and 8.b.
46. Aircraft captains are responsible for briefing the ground crew on start requirements and 
ancillary operations prior to pulling the chocks. Caution must be taken when starting aircraft at 
U.S. bases with external power to ensure that start carts meet requirements as per appropriate 
Aircraft Operation Checklist (similar looking carts may have reverse polarity).
Flight over Large Bodies of Water – Harvard II
47. Harvard II Pilots shall, at all times, be within gliding distance of shore when flying over 
large bodies of open water (e.g. the Great Lakes, Georgia Straits, etc.) These restrictions do not 
apply while conducting IFR Approach Procedures or deviating around significant weather.
Weekend Recovery Window Procedures
48. Aircrew will recover in Moose Jaw during the published weekend recovery window. If 
prior to the weekend recovery window becoming active aircrew will be unable to arrive in 
Moose Jaw, they must contact the 2 CFFTS Duty Ops Officer ASAP. The Duty Ops O will then 
advise the 15 Wing Ops O. If the recovery window is active and aircrew will be unable to arrive 
in Moose Jaw, they must contact the 15 Wing Tower at 306-694-2222 ext 5574 and advise them 
directly.
Upon Return to Moose Jaw
49. Credit cards and aircraft keys shall be returned to the applicable servicing desk.
50. Issued cell phones, tablets, and additional signed out kit shall be returned to the Duty Ops 
O. If returning during a weekend recovery window or whenever the Ops desk is not manned by 
the Duty Ops O, the signed-out items will need to be returned on the next working day. The 
phones are not to be left on the Ops desk, where they are not secure.
51. United States pubs shall be returned to appropriate pubs cabinet if applicable.
Customs Procedures 
52. Customs services are available at Moose Jaw. Refer to 15 WFO for current procedures.
53. Pilots entering Canada without having made the specified arrangements shall clear 
customs at another AOE prior to flying to Moose Jaw.
54. Aircrew arriving at Grand Forks International (KGFK) or Hector International in Fargo, 
ND (KFAR) are to follow the instructions located in Annex J. Aircrew arriving at Minneapolis 
St. Paul (KMSP) are also to follow the instructions in Annex J.

127
Pilot Servicing Inspections
55. All 2 CFFTS QFIs shall maintain a valid qualification for pilot servicing inspections. 
After initial trg the qualification will be valid for 12 months. By the end of the 12th month staff 
must renew the qualification by attending refresher trg. Appropriate forms for aircraft type are 
located at Annex K.
56. Refresher trg will occur the first Tuesday of every month or if booked with servicing. 
Aircrew shall report to the Ops Desk at 0900 hrs. 
TD Expenses
57. Accommodations. To prevent the practice of upgrading hotels because of lack of 
availability, accommodations shall be booked as part of the cross-country / TD planning process. 
However, it is recognized that this may not be possible for “last minute” TD. “White page” 
accommodations shall be used unless otherwise approved by the Cmdt. Hotels with free shuttle 
services shall be used if they are available and the hotel either contains dining facilities or is 
within close proximity (walking distance) of dining facilities. If accommodations used exceed 
the entitlements specified in CFTDTI, justification must be provided prior to Cmdt consideration 
of reimbursement. Justification would include attending a conference or training offered within a 
hotel. If white pages accommodations are available for an airport location, the airport white page 
maximum will be authorized, i.e. if members fly a quarterly proficiency trip to Vancouver, they 
will be reimbursed for the maximum white page value for the airport location, even if they elect 
to stay at downtown white page accommodations. As a basic standard, members are entitled to 
individual room. This is to minimize disruption to sleep patterns. Members may share a suite, 
providing separate rooms are available.
58. Taxis. Members shall not be reimbursed for taxis if a free shuttle service was available; 
however, if the shuttle service does not service the FBO or is not available within a one hour 
waiting period, a taxi may be used. If the accommodations are not serviced by a free shuttle 
service, the nearest hotel listed in white pages that has dining facilities (or is within close 
proximity of dining facilities), and at which the member(s) could have stayed, shall be used to 
establish the maximum amount that will be reimbursed for taxi costs. Members using military 
accommodations who arrive after meal hours or are departing before meal hours may be 
reimbursed reasonable taxi costs to and from a dining facility. This will be on a “per case” basis 
and approved by the Cmdt. In the case where white page accommodations are available at the 
airport of arrival and the members chose to stay at another location, ground transportation shall 
only be reimbursed if there was no free airport shuttle service available to white page airport 
accommodations. In this case, the maximum reimbursement will be the cost of transportation 
from the airport to the nearest white page airport accommodations.
59. Rental Vehicles. A rental vehicle shall only be used if it is going to be the most 
economical means of transportation. In the case where the distance to the nearest suitable “white 
page” accommodations is excessive and no free shuttle service is available, a rental vehicle may 
be the most economical means of transportation. Likewise, if a shuttle service charges individual 
fees, the number of 2 CFFTS personnel requiring transport may make the use of a rental vehicle 

128
the more economical option. In all cases, all costs must be considered, including parking, fuel 
and taxes. Rental vehicles will normally be requested and approved prior to travel.
60. Itinerary Changes. It is recognized that changes occur due to weather, interruptions to 
commercial flight service, etc. If travel plans change, members must provide corroboration to 
justify any expenses claimed for reimbursement. For example, in the event that a diversion was 
required, printed wx briefings and an explanation shall be attached to the TD claim. If stranded 
because of a change to commercial flight service, highway closure or travel advisory, 
documentation in the form of a printed itinerary change or internet advisory shall be attached to 
the claim. NOTE: Accommodation charges incurred because of itinerary changes forced by 
weather or operational considerations may be reimbursed with Cmdt’s authorization. In no case 
will reimbursement be considered if the member(s) could reasonably have cancelled the booking
or if the itinerary change was optional.
61. Cross-Country Sorties to Victoria. The Victoria Airport is located in Sidney, BC; 
approximately 26 km from Victoria. Members going cross-country to Victoria may stay in 
Sidney and taxi costs will be reimbursed if a shuttle is not available. Members staying in Victoria 
shall use military accommodations; if they are not available, commercial accommodations may 
be used provided a cost-free shuttle is used. Members may stay in private accommodations, but 
transport will not be reimbursed. Members who stay in military accommodations will be 
authorized the use of a rental vehicle.
62. RON Out-and-Back Sorties to Edmonton International Airport. Members who fly RON 
out and back sorties to Edmonton and land at Edmonton International shall be reimbursed up to 
the maximum white page accommodations rate for Leduc. Ground transportation shall only be 
reimbursed in the event that no free shuttle service is available. In this case, the maximum 
reimbursement will be the cost of transportation from the airport to the white page 
accommodations in Leduc.
Meals / Taxis – Non-RON Out-and-Backs
63. 2 CFFTS frequently conducts out-and-back training over a meal hour and members are 
entitled to claim the expense of the meal, up to the approved amount, if receipts are provided. 
However, if there is a restaurant available on the airport within walking distance or to which the 
FBO will provide free transport, there is no entitlement for commercial transportation. If 
members decide to dine elsewhere, they are responsible for any costs incurred for transportation.
Diversions to Local Alternates
64. Turn / Recovery. If aircraft are forced to divert to a local alternate for any reason, upon 
landing, the pilot in command will immediately telephone Ops, update aircraft status and await 
instructions. Aircrew shall turn the aircraft as soon as possible and be prepared to RTB 
immediately. Aircrew shall not depart the FSS / FBO unless cleared to do so by Ops. Aircrew 
shall not fly an unplanned sortie on the RTB leg unless approved by Ops.

129
65. Meals. If aircrew could have returned prior to the end of a meal hour, there is no 
entitlement to claim a meal.
Syllabus Missions at TD Locations
66. On a per case basis, the Cmdt may approve student syllabus missions to be flown at 
another location. The Adjt will scrutinize these types of missions to ensure funding is available. 
QFIs requesting approval for such missions must plan on flying two student missions per day 
while on temporary duty and the destination should be within one leg of Moose Jaw.
Airshow Participation
67. 2 CFFTS will provide static displays to airshows as tasked by 1 Cdn Air Div. QFIs shall 
use proficiency hours for transit. One non-working day shall be allocated for every two full days 
working static displays IAW the intent of 1 Cdn Air Div Orders, Vol 2, 2-002, paras 44 – 46. 
Ops will track airshow participation and attempt to make distribution as fair as possible. QFIs 
shall not normally be tasked to go to more than one airshow per year nor shall they normally be 
tasked to go the same airshow more than once during their tour at 2 CFFTS. Exceptions shall 
require Cmdt approval.
Financial Reimbursement
68. Student Syllabus and Staff Proficiency CrossCountry. Approval of the proposed itinerary 
IAW 2 CFFTS Flying Orders shall constitute authority to claim all reasonable and actual 
expenses as authorized by regulations and local restrictions.
TD Costs and Expenditure Recording
69. To facilitate the control and handling of TD funds, the following procedures will be 
followed:
a. Currently, claims are not raised prior to TD trips funded by 2 CFFTS. This is due 
to the reality that most of these trips are scheduled with little notice, and that 
many changes typically occur. 
b. Once the member returns from the trip, they are required to complete a TD 
worksheet, and attach all receipts. The claim will be raised by the clerks, and will 
usually be completed and ready for the claimant’s signature within a 24-hour 
period.
c. Original copies of claims are to be filed and locked each night and maintained in 
the FTS OR. Claims will be moved to the 15 Wing Archive Room after 2 years 
and marked for deletion after 7 years due to GST rules and regulations.
70. The following signature blocks shall be completed as follows:

130
a. Block A – “Signature of Commanding Officer” portion will be signed by the 2 
CFFTS Adjutant or the CFI for the Commandant. 
b. Sections 32 and 34 of the claim will be signed by the AdminO or Chief Clerk. If 
neither is available, then Sections 32 and 34 may be signed by anyone who has 
completed the required certification, and been delegated written authority from 
the Commandant.
71. All claims using a Financial Code other than that of 2 CFFTS will be raised by Wg OR 
staff, providing there is a hard copy of the authorization for the TD and use of the financial 
coding. This information will be attached to the claim when finalized. In addition, the OR staff 
will ensure a copy of the claim is forwarded to the appropriate RC Manager for their retention.
Budget information is maintained by the AdminO, who in turn makes bi-weekly reports to the 
Commandant on the financial situation of the unit.
Maintenix/FlightPro sign out/in
72. For any approved Trip Request, Aircraft Commanders will work with the Duty OpsO to 
build their planned departure strip, weekend flying strips, and arrival strip(s) in FlightPro. On 
RTB, they will work with the Duty OpsO to ensure that all entries are correct and accounted for 
to alleviate missed entries on Maintenix. This is to alleviate aircraft being overflown, typically 
when a flight is missed. Historically, this is usually from not clicking “OK” in Maintenix to 
finalize the flight. NOTE: during Maintenix sign-in of flights, if and ECTM was accomplished, 
allow the Servicing Desk to complete that for the flight it occurred on, before continuing to sign 
in flights. In all cases, Pilots are to verify under the historical flights tab after signing planes in to 
Maintenix to confirm all flights were entered properly.

BOOK 3 – TRAINING ORDERS
CHAPTER 1 – GENERAL
Standards Internal Audit Terms of Reference
Ref: AOT SET Terms of Reference
1. 15 Wg Stds shall annually evaluate all aspects of the flying, academic, and officer 
development programs. They will assess and report on the operation of the following:
a. Chief Flying Instructor's Organization;
b. Ground Based Training Academic Training;
c. Ground Based Training – Flight Training Devices;
d. 2 CFFTS Operations;
e. 2 CFFTS Standards; and,
f. Flying Instructor and related training.
2 CFFTS Language of Instruction Policy
Ref: 1211-1 (D Air Pers Mgt) Application of the Official Languages Policy within the Royal 
Canadian Air Force Training System dated 03 Feb 13
2. It is required that all students reporting for flight training at 2 CFFTS have met the 
minimum English language proficiency requirement as stipulated in the Integrated Training Plan 
(ITP). 
3. Ph II and III ground and airborne training will be conducted in English only as these 
phases are considered “advanced training that requires exercising manoeuvres and techniques in 
real time” as defined in ref. As such the following will be provided: 
a. training aids, reference materials, précis and handouts will be in English;
b. tests and examinations will be given in English; and,
c. in-flight instruction will be conducted in English. All students are to be 
continually monitored by their instructors and Flt Comds for any weaknesses or 
lack of comprehension. Any language issues will be reported to the CoC and a 
specific course of action will be developed to assist these students. 

132
4. 2 CFFTS will ensure the following assistance is available to aid with English language 
comprehension difficulties: 
a. an additional ten minutes for every hour allotted to complete ground school tests 
or examinations will be given. Additionally, every effort will be made to offer 
assistance in understanding examination questions;
b. a minimum of one CD shall be bilingual (French/English); and
c. a minimum of one QFI should be bilingual (French/English) in each flight.
5. All Staff are to be familiar with the program and fully aware that nothing in the directive 
precludes giving assistance in every form possible to overcome problems which a student may be 
experiencing. The implementation of this program should provide the necessary means to 
instructors and supervisors for dealing with specific comprehension problems caused by 
language difficulties. In case of queries or if specific problems arise in carrying out the program, 
the appropriate supervisor is to be informed and consulted as soon as possible.
Course Director and Student Course Overview
6. All information relating to course administration can be found in the Course Directors’ 
Guide located in Annex L.

133
CHAPTER 2 – FLIGHT LINE OFFICER DEVELOPMENT ASSESSMENT (FLODA)
Ref: 1 Cdn Air Div Orders, Vol 5, 5-108
1. Flt Comds are to maintain a FLODA Program within their Flt that will provide each 
student with opportunities to develop as an Officer. Student pilots including Occupational 
Transfers (OTs) below the rank of Captain will attend formal OD classes. OTs who are the rank 
of Captain may attend the formal OD classes and sessions on a voluntary basis. Students under 
the rank of Captain will be assessed and debriefed on their performance using the criteria 
outlined in the FLODA evaluation form on FlightPro. It is crucial that when duties are assigned 
to students that they be completed to the best of their abilities. Although OTs may not have flight 
line secondary duties, they will be expected to conduct themselves in a manner commensurate 
with their level of experience as an officer and independently display the “Officer-Like 
Qualities” listed in the 2 CFFTS Orders. OTs and students must be debriefed in sufficient time to 
allow an opportunity to improve upon poor performance. Flt Comds will ensure that assessments 
(FLODA) are completed on each student and submitted to the appropriate CD IAW the 
following guidelines:
a. Ph II. – first FLODA completed after completing the IIT, second FLODA (endcourse FLODA) completed after the fourth flight test;
b. Ph III. – one end-course FLODA completed four weeks prior to end of course; 
2. FLODA scores shall be provided in the following manner:
a. Ph II. – CDs shall provide the mid-course OD score for students on their 
respective courses. Should Deputy Flt Comds wish to amend this score, they shall 
consult with the CD whereupon an agreed score shall be determined. End course 
OD scores shall be provided by Deputy Flt Comds. Should the CD wish to amend 
this score they shall consult with the Deputy Flt Comd whereupon an agreed score 
shall be determined;
b. Ph III. – Deputy Flt Comds shall provide the end course OD score for students. 
Should CDs wish to amend this score, they shall consult with the Deputy Flt 
Comd whereupon an agreed score shall be determined; and 
3. To assist in the completion of the FLODA, 12 assessment factors are defined in Annex L
with the applicable rating levels.
4. Undergraduate Aircrew Significant Activity Report (UASAR). UASARs (Annex M) will 
be used by all QFIs to report noteworthy (both good and bad) OD activity and performance. 
When completed, these forms shall be forwarded to the applicable CD for administration and 
subsequent retention on the student's Aircrew Training File.
Canadian Students’ Unsatisfactory Officer Development 

134
Ref: A-PD-055-002/PP-003, Canadian Armed Forces Military Employment Structure (MES), 
Volume 2 – General Specifications, Part 3 – Officer General Specifications
5. During their stay at 2 CFFTS, Canadian student pilots, including Occupational Transfers 
(OTs) are required to achieve qualification in Officer Development (OD). In order to achieve this 
qualification, the candidate must:
a. successfully complete performance requirements for the appropriate rank level as 
identified in ref;
b. display the following “Officer Like Qualities” (OLQs):
(1) integrity – the candidate is just, honest, and honourable towards superiors, 
peers, and subordinates;
(2) loyalty – the candidate is faithful to superiors, subordinates, and peers;
(3) exemplary conduct – the candidate’s personal behaviour, both on and off 
duty, reflect credit upon him/herself and the CF;
(4) dedication – the candidate perseveres under trying circumstances to 
complete the mission or task; and
(5) courage – the candidate resists the temptation to abandon ideals or 
objectives in the face of danger or hardship.
c. demonstrate leadership qualities and a positive attitude towards training, 
participation, and teamwork; and,
d. display standards of dress and deportment IAW current regulations.
6. Identification of Unsatisfactory Progress. To document incidents that indicate that a 
practical OD problem exists with a student or that a problem is developing, the following 
procedures will apply:
a. Stage I- Counselling for Officer Development Offences
(1) a minor offence will be recorded on a UASAR (Annex M) The CD/Flt 
Comd will counsel the student on the requirement for more appropriate 
behaviour; 
(2) a major offence will result in a formal debrief. The student will then be 
formally warned using the Statement of Formal Warning (Annex N);

135
(3) a serious offence will result in an IR or PRB at the discretion of the Cmdt 
to determine what administrative and/or disciplinary actions will be 
initiated;
(4) the repetition of a minor offence within a three month period may be 
considered a major offence;
(5) the accumulation of three minor offences may be considered a major 
offence; and
(6) UASARs and/or Statements of Formal Warning are to be sent to the Adjt 
for inclusion on the student’s training file.
b. Stage II- OD IR
(1) an IR will be conducted whenever the candidate has been rated Marginal 
on a FLODA; or, the student is reported for misconduct at any time after 
having been formally counselled and placed on formal warning.
(2) the purpose of the OD IR is to ensure that the student was handled 
properly and in accordance with applicable regulations and to confirm if a 
PRB may warranted. The IR will report its findings and make 
recommendations to the Cmdt.
(3) The IR may recommend that a PRB is to be convened; or, other 
disciplinary measures in lieu of referral to a PRB.
c. Stage III- PRB for OD
(1) the Flt Comd will inform the Cmdt and recommend that a PRB be 
convened for the following reasons:
(a) upon the recommendation of an IR;
(b) if a student is rated Unsatisfactory or Marginal on a FLODA.
(2) the Cmdt has the right to order a PRB for OD at any time they feels it is 
warranted.
(3) the Cmdt shall determine the action to be taken based on the 
recommendations from the PRB. The PRB may make recommendations 
on at least one or more of the following:
(a) Initial Counselling (IC);
(b) Recorded Warning (RW);

136
(c) Charge IAW the National Defence Act; and/or,
(d) Cease Training (CT).

137
CHAPTER 3 – STUDENT PHYSICAL DEVELOPMENT AND TRAINING (PD&C)
Student PD&C Policy
1. Physical and Mental health are critical to successful pilot training. Good nutrition, health, 
and stress relief are small things that will help develop resiliency and help with recovery from 
illness and injuries. The guidelines for mandatory PD&C are as follows:
a. Mandatory 12 credits a month. Activities are described in Para 2, 3 and 4.
b. 8 credits shall be physical activity sessions and 2 shall be mental performance. 
The remaining 2 credits are flex credits where a student can attend mental 
performance sessions or additional physical activity sessions. 
c. If a Flt Comd or CD deems that a student’s fitness is not sufficient they will be 
required to report their sessions to their applicable supervisor. Further deficiency 
shall be recorded with a UASAR or in exceptional cases a remedial measure.
d. In the event that a student becomes concerned about the ability to maintain their 
body weight within set aircrew limitations, the member shall immediately advise 
their CD, and seek assistance from PSP to acquire a dietary and physical fitness 
program that will effectively manage and monitor their body weight.
e. If a student fails to maintain seat weight and are ordered onto a fitness regimen, 
they will be required to report their sessions to their supervisor.
f. Students shall inform their respective CD of the sessions they’ve attended to 
achieve 12 credits a month.
g. If a student fails to achieve 12 credits a month, their respective CD will inform the 
Adj. Once assigned to a flight, the CD will also inform the respective Flight 
Comd and Deputy Flight Comd.
2. The following activities are considered CF approved physical fitness activities for 
PD&C: Aircrew Conditioning Program(ACP); Walking; Jogging; Stationary cycling (Spinning 
included); Cycling; Skating; Snowshoeing; Swimming; Rope skipping; Cross-country skiing; 
Muscular strength and endurance (Weight training); Elliptical; Stairmaster/Step mill; and 
Rowing.
3. The following activities are considered CF approved sporting activities for PD&C:
Badminton; Squash; Hockey; Ball Hockey/Floor Hockey; Volleyball; Basketball; Soccer; 
Triathlon; Running; and, Taekwondo.
4. Mental performance sessions are available through the Mental Performance coach. These 
can range from monthly workshops, 1:1 sessions, to homework assignments.

138
5. “Extreme sports” also called action sports and adventure sports, are used to describe 
certain activities perceived as having a high level of inherent risk or difficulty and often involve 
speed, height, a high level of physical exertion, and highly specialized equipment.
6. Extreme sports and any activities that are not mentioned in the above lists will need to 
receive approval from the WComd via out of service approval forms. 
Prevention of Sports Injuries
7. To help prevent expensive, serious injuries, students shall not participate in body contact 
sports or sports with a high injury risk factor unless that particular sport is closely supervised. 
NOTE: Closely supervised sports mean that each student must be briefed on the limitations of 
body contact and on type of play permitted and that close observation of the actual play is carried 
out to ensure the limitations are not exceeded.
8. The following sports are prohibited to students undergoing training at 2 CFFTS unless 
specific permission has been obtained from the Commandant 2 CFFTS: parachuting; hang 
gliding; bungee jumping; ultra light flying; rugby; extreme combat activities (ultimate fighting, 
mixed martial arts); extreme winter sports (luge, skeleton, ski jumping); and motorized sporting 
competitions (rally/ motor racing, motocross).
9. It is highly recommended to avoid any sport where even small injuries are possible. A 
small wrist or ankle injury can have a massive impact on training time. 

139
CHAPTER 4 – STUDENT FLIGHT LINE CONDUCT
Policy Regarding Breakfasts
1. All students shall consume an adequate breakfast if they are flying or on duty in the 
morning.
Bad Weather Program
2. Flt Comds shall appoint a QFI to act as Bad Weather Program Co-ordinator. The 
appointed QFI will liaise with the Flt Trg O to organize appropriate continuation training 
lectures, or recreational activities. 
3. The CFI is to monitor/supervise the efficient use of Flying Training down time.
4. Standards is responsible for creating an electronic repository of bad weather briefs 
located at //Hercules/Big 2 Bad Weather Program/.
Mass Briefings
5. Flt Comds will ensure a briefing is provided to new students on their flight line 
responsibilities, including mission planning, preparation, student work day etc.
6. PH2. CD is to ensure, in coordination with GBTS, the delivery of the CH and IF mass 
briefs are completed prior to commencing the FTD training. The CD will also coordinate with 
Stds for the delivery of the emergency handling mass brief during the scheduled time.
7. PH2 and 3 Hvd. Flt Comds shall ensure that the students receive a phase brief prior to the 
commencement of the remaining phases of flying.
8. Standards is responsible for maintaining the electronic library of phase briefings.
Student Flying Training Day
9. Student's Crew Duty Day (CDD) CDD commences when they report for flying duties and 
ceases when they are released from flying duties (this includes pre and post-flight activities such 
as briefings/debriefings and other flying related activities). A Student Pilot's CDD shall not 
exceed 12 hours. Remaining within the school building to conduct activities that include, but are 
not limited to, practice FTDs and/or to prepare navigation maps does not count as part of the 
Crew Duty Day (CDD).
10. A Student Pilot's Crew Rest (CR) begins when a they are released from flying duties in 
preparation for the next CDD.

140
11. Students are expected to be prepared for two events in each phase of flight. They are 
expected to conduct two events per training day. A single event is defined as any of the 
following:
a. three to four classes of ground school;
b. an FTD (not including student-led practice FTD sessions); and
c. a flying mission.
12. By exception, Flt Comds may authorize a third event if required but must annotate 
progress cards indicating the justification. With the exception of CH8A in Phase II, the third 
event will not be a solo flight.
13. Crew Duty Day commences when the student reports for duty and ceases when they is 
released from duty (this includes mission and weather briefings, maintenance and other delays). 
14. Students should not normally work weekends. However, this may be required at the 
discretion of the Cmdt. In such cases, students can work continuously for a maximum of seven 
days, at which point they must be awarded at least one day off before returning to duties.
Clearhood Phase
15. Prior to flying the first solo (Phase II only), students must fly a solo check within the 
previous 24 hours. Further, all items found in Annex W must be completed.
16. If circumstances do not allow for a student solo after the initial Solo Check, at the 
discretion of the Flt Comd, the first solo may be flown after the next syllabus CH mission (CH9 
for Ph II).
17. Senior QFIs will conduct the initial CH solo check as well as the Ph III Harvard NT solo 
check. This includes missions that are considered to count as the initial solo check (CH7 in 
special cases, CH8ED, CH8RV (Ph II) or CH9 if the solo could not be accomplished on the same 
day as CH8).
18. Students shall be supervised during strap in for the first solo mission. A QFI will be 
available either at Ops or in the Tower during a student’s first solo in case of any problems. The 
duty Ops O can fill this requirement provided they are a QFI.
Instrument Phase
19. When conditions permit, QFIs should seek opportunities to expose their students to IMC 
flying. 
20. Phase 2 Instrument Flying aims to establish a basic instrument crosscheck. Phase 2 
students will conduct their instrument training from the rear seat. The front seat QFI will conduct 

141
all take-offs and landings. The student is not responsible for lookout, VFR traffic deconfliction, 
or any other restrictions specific to IFR operations in VMC and require visual reference to the 
ground or other aircraft. The QFI must clarify airborne responsibilities in the briefing.
21. Phase 2 Instrument Flying students should make planning and airborne decisions based 
on the actual weather conditions, but are tasked by their instructor to conduct specific tasks, such 
as stabilized approaches to the published minima. Upon reaching approach minima or the missed 
approach point, the student must call “MDA”, “DA”, or “Missed Approach Point” as 
appropriate. Unless the instructor takes control, the student will initiate the corresponding missed 
approach.
22. Phase 3 Instrument Flying includes conducting IFR procedures in VMC. Phase 3 students 
will fly from the front seat and are responsible for take-offs, landings, lookout, traffic 
deconfliction, and any additional VMC restrictions or factors. As with Phase 2, students should 
make planning and airborne decisions based on the actual weather conditions. Instructors will 
task students to conduct specific tasks, such as stabilized approaches to published minima. Once 
the instructor calls “visual”, the student may proceed with a landing transition. Otherwise, the 
student will carry out the missed approach once at DH or the MAP. 
Formation Phase
23. Flt Comds may authorize students as rear seat passengers on syllabus formation lead 
missions, provided the student is post-solo, is not in the formation phase, and has been properly 
briefed on pertinent aspects of formation flying, specifically hand signals. This does not prohibit 
non-aircrew passengers from flying on syllabus formation missions provided they have been 
properly briefed and have received the authority to fly as a passenger IAW these orders. Students 
will not log any flying time when flying as a passenger on syllabus formation missions.
24. During formation missions, at least one QFI who holds an Element Lead qualification 
shall be present within the formation. The pilot flying the dedicated lead for the student solos or 
any EDs shall be an Element Lead qualified QFI. 
EFTD Phase
25. FTD/EFTD currency requirements and review EFTD authorizations will be managed 
IAW PH2 Training Plan (Ref: RCAF Training Plan – Version 3.0 Ph II – RCAF CT-156 (Hvd 
II) Pilot, Annex H1-8) or IAW PH3 Training Plan (Ref: RCAF Training Plan – Version 3.0 Ph 
III – RCAF CT-156 (Hvd II) Pilot, Annex H1-10). Flight Commander approval authorities and 
conditions for review EFTDs are as specified in the TP.
26. For Phase II, if the last EFTD completed is EFTD1 and the student has not gone solo yet, 
the additional EFTD will be created as EFTDRE1 as this sortie is the specific pre-solo EFTD. If 
the student has completed the first solo, the next EFTD in FlightPro is to be utilized. EFTD4RE’s 
are to be created for any additional EFTD’s required after the use of the four that currently exist 
in the ITP/FlightPro course flow. 

142
27. As per the ITP, the failure of any EFTD will result in Unsatisfactory FTD Progress.
Ejection Seat Trainer
28. Students must have undergone NFTC seat ejection training prior to being allowed to fly.
29. As part of the ground school syllabus, all students will undergo initial seat training prior 
to commencement of flying training. Once on the flight line, it is the students’ responsibility to 
ensure that they undergo refresher training as per below.
30. Student pilots shall receive Ejection Training every 30 days. When the Ejection Training 
is completed within a 10 calendar day period prior to the expiry date, the training currency will 
be valid for 30 days from the original expiry date (e.g., expiry date 30 Aug, training done on 21 
Aug, new expiry date is 30 Sep). This currency will be updated and monitored in FlightPro.
Harvard Static Trainer
31. The following procedures will apply for use of the Harvard aircraft for a static trainer. 
Students must be on course, have completed, as a minimum, FTD Cockpit Procedures Trainer #1 
(CPT-1), 1 Egress trainer, and the student must have received a thorough briefing on safety in 
and around the aircraft while in use of the static trainer by GBTS staff.
32. In addition, students are to report to the team lead running the servicing desk prior to, and 
when finished using the static aircraft and must inform the team lead of the type of training to be 
conducted in the aircraft.
Flight Training Device (FTD)
33. When performing training in any FTD, pilots shall fasten and tighten the lap belt to avoid 
damage to the cable and pulley system. While adjusting the seat height, ensure that no loose 
items or parts of the FTD jam between the seat and the side console panels.
34. FTD missions should normally be completed following the established ITP syllabus 
sequence. Deviations from the ITP syllabus sequence are authorized within the following policy 
guidelines:
a. Flt Comds are authorized to approve the completion of an additional two FTD 
missions beyond the FTD mission which follows the last completed flying sortie. 
The “Out of Sequence FTD” workflow must be signed off and attached to the 
prog card of the out-of-sequence mission(s).
b. Before authorizing syllabus deviations, Flt Comds shall consider the following 
factors:
(1) Students experiencing any significant difficulties in the phase in question 

143
or overall on the course should not normally deviate from the ITP course 
flow; 
(2) If the amount and/ or degree of complexity of “new work” or upgrade 
items in the out-of-sequence FTDs will likely pose problems for the 
student in terms of achieving the desired proficiency, the syllabus
